#### Abstracts
##### **AI-Driven Reproducibility and Analytical Robustness in Scientific Discovery**
2610.23845v1 by Shicheng Liu, Krista Opsahl-Ong, Andrew Drozdov, Julian Killingback, Arnav Singhvi, Ronak Pradeep, Michael Bendersky, James C. Dickerson, Monica S. Lam

Reproducible and analytically robust research findings are essential to scientific progress, but evaluating these properties typically requires costly manual effort. We investigate whether LLM agents can help automate reproduction and robustness analysis, using a medical case study grounded in the Surveillance, Epidemiology, and End Results (SEER) database, a widely used U.S. cancer registry underlying tens of thousands of published studies. First, we introduce SEER-Repro, a benchmark of 20 published SEER-based papers together with the data and resources required to reproduce their findings. Second, we create an AI agent that successfully reproduces 77.4% of 2,190 atomic claims, while providing executable baselines for further analysis. Third, we develop an agentic analysis pipeline to test the robustness of the medical findings by identifying analytical choices underlying each finding, proposing defensible alternatives, and rerunning the analysis to determine whether those choices affect the conclusion. Across published papers in SEER-Repro, our pipeline identifies potentially consequential analytical choices in 9.5% of fully reproduced findings and flags them for expert review. These results show how LLM agents can support scientific review by reproducing findings, systematically testing their robustness, and directing expert attention toward potentially consequential analytical choices.

摘要：

##### **On the possibilities and limitations of LLM-based multi-agent systems for multi-criteria decision-making in healthcare operations management**
2610.23673v1 by Marco Boresta, Tommaso Giovannelli, Sepehr Namirad, Giulia Riccardi

Multi-criteria models for healthcare operations need weights that express how stakeholders trade off patient waiting time, provider workload, and operating cost, but these weights are difficult to quantify. Teams of large language model (LLM) agents, each prompted to represent a stakeholder, have been used to supply them. We propose a framework for assessing the reliability of LLM-generated preferences as inputs to multi-objective optimization and quantifying how their uncertainty affects decisions. We study explicit preferences by asking agents to rate the importance of the criteria in an outpatient appointment scheduling problem, and implicit preferences by inferring trade-offs from the agents' pairwise choices between emergency department configurations. The computational experiments show that the explicit preferences respond to the clinic's operating conditions and differ across roles, but deliberation among the agents can erase these differences, whereas simple averaging preserves them. The pairwise choices used to reveal the agents' implicit preferences depend largely on which option is presented first, and we find limited evidence that a statistical model using contextual information alongside presentation order predicts the agents' choices on new comparison pairs better than a model using presentation order alone. We then formulate robust weighted-sum optimization models to account for preference uncertainty. In most cases, these models select different schedules or configurations from those obtained using average preference weights. These results indicate that LLM-generated preferences need an audit of this kind before they are used as optimization weights in healthcare decision support.

摘要：

##### **Self-Reflective Chain-of-Thought (SRCoT): Evidence-Based Text Generation via a Closed-Loop Retrieve--Reflect--Revise Pipeline on Edge LLMs**
2610.23311v1 by Fabian Broszio, Michael Hoffmann, Adrian Paschke

Large language models (LLMs) can generate fluent outputs that lack source support or conflict with local operational constraints. We introduce Self-Reflective Chain-of-Thought (SRCoT), a modular architecture that organizes generation as a closed Retrieve--Reflect--Revise loop. SRCoT separates environment state, persistent knowledge, accumulated experience, and short-term memory into provenance-labeled stores. BM25 retrieval supplies local context; subsequent passes expose intermediate drafts, missing-information markers, follow-up queries, and optional claim--evidence mappings. These artifacts inform retrieval, revision, clarification, or abstention decisions. The traces also reveal provenance-assignment errors, incomplete evidence coverage, and unsafe assumptions that remain possible despite prompting constraints. These cases demonstrate report-level inspectability and local execution, not comparative superiority, hallucination reduction, clinical validity, or operational safety. SRCoT is a research proof of concept requiring human review, with its principal contribution lying in an inspectable control procedure rather than a new model.

##### **The Self-Improvement Problem**
2610.21646v1 by Brian B. Moser, Stanislav Frolov, Tobias C. Nauen, Federico Raue, Andreas Dengel

With the rise of large language models, agentic systems are becoming increasingly autonomous, as they write code, call tools, revise prompts, and propose modifications to their own components. This autonomy raises a fundamental question for safe self-improvement: can an automated gate like a reward model or an automated reviewer reliably decide whether a proposed update should be accepted? The difficulty is structural. The gate must be a terminating, computable procedure, while the property we ultimately care about, namely whether an update is a genuine semantic improvement, depends on potentially unbounded program behavior. We formalize this mismatch by modeling agents as programs and defining improvement in terms of a semantic score that need not be computable. We study two evaluation abstractions. The first is a certifier that approves or rejects an update. The second is a search procedure that, given access to a proposal generator, attempts to output some improving candidate if one exists. We show that, in the fully general setting, no terminating procedure can simultaneously guarantee reliability (never accepting a non-improvement) and non-conservatism (never missing an available improvement): self-referential constructions can force any such gate or searcher to fail. Randomized gates with bounded error face the same limit. We then identify a standard decidable regime used in practice (bounded evaluation and a finite proposal budget) under which exhaustive simulation yields a terminating search procedure with the desired guarantees. We also show the limit of this recovery: a chain of accepted updates can raise the bounded score in every round while the true score falls. Our results draw a boundary between unbounded, universal settings where correctness guarantees collapse and bounded settings where they can be recovered by design.

##### **Agent Status Protocol (ASP): A Lightweight Specification for State Reporting in Multi-Agent Systems**
2610.21538v1 by Diego Gosmar, Deborah A. Dahl, Emmett Coin, Csaba Bolyós, Andreas Zettl

The Agent Status Protocol (ASP) defines a lightweight way for heterogeneous AI agents to report their operational state to a status server, and for observers such as dashboards to receive that state as soon as it changes. Agents push small JSON events over HTTP POST; the server exposes the aggregated state to observers over Server-Sent Events (SSE). Because an agent that has crashed, hung or been compromised cannot be trusted to report its own failure, ASP does not rely on self-reports alone: the server infers liveness from missed heartbeats, can detect agents that are alive but no longer making progress, and accepts observations from supervisors, guardrails, operators and peers, some of which take precedence over what the agent says about itself while others are shown alongside it. ASP models state along independent dimensions (lifecycle, health, task, floor, governance), so that an agent can be, for example, online, degraded, working on a task and waiting for the floor at the same time. It reuses existing vocabularies: task states from the Agent2Agent (A2A) protocol, floor states from the Open Floor Protocol (OFP), and attribute conventions from the OpenTelemetry GenAI semantic conventions. This paper presents version 0.1 of ASP as a proposal, not as an approved standard. It is a working draft intended to open a discussion, and the authors invite comments, alternative proposals and independent implementations from the community.

Before the solver trains on a generated task, an adversary writes an answer that games its rubric while being as useless as possible, the same model writes an honest answer without seeing the rubric, and up to two repair steps revise the rubric to make the honest answer win. The agents' collaboration is governed by two novel adaptive coordination mechanisms: (1) Dynamic Self-Updating Weight Adjustment (DSWA), a long-term mechanism that adjusts the influence weights of agents based on historical case outcomes, enabling the system to adapt and trust more reliable specialists over time. (2) Discrepancy Feedback Precise Calibration (DFPC), a short-term mechanism where agents provide iterative feedback to resolve disagreements and eliminate prescription conflicts for the current patient. Experiments on patient records from the MIMIC-IV database show that PRISMATIC significantly reduces drug conflicts, improves prescription coverage, and enhances treatment consistency compared to strong LLM baselines. Our work demonstrates that structuring clinical co-treatment as a multi-agent coordination problem offers a promising path toward safer and more coherent multidisease management.

Existing monitoring methods typically control false alarms by requiring the discrepancy measure used to detect shifts to be *pre-specified*. However, for unstructured data, pre-specification creates a sensitivity--breadth trade-off: a narrowly defined measure is sensitive to the anticipated shift but may miss unexpected changes, whereas a broadly defined measure may detect a wider range of shifts but with reduced sensitivity. Although adaptively hypothesizing the shift direction can overcome this limitation, such data-dependent adaptation generally invalidates conventional false-alarm guarantees. We introduce *asymptotic e-detectors*, which approximate the pre-change distribution using a calibration set of size $m$, and prove that they asymptotically control the average run length to a false alarm as $m$ increases. Empirically, calibration sets as small as $m=30$ provide effective control. We then develop adaptive monitoring strategies ranging from black-box bettors that learn from embeddings to interpretable bettors based on LLM-proposed concepts. Further, when the true shifts are sparse and interpretable, LLM-concept monitors provide the fastest and most interpretable detection, while also guiding root-cause diagnosis for complex shifts.

EEG is not an arbitrary multichannel signal: each channel records the voltage difference between two scalp electrodes. So, real recordings satisfy exact linear identities among their channels. We therefore propose MontageFlow, a flow-matching model that enforces these identities by restricting its output to signals that satisfy them. We find that ensuring validity improves fidelity too. When asked what would have happened if a doctor had not recommended surgery, the model output should retain the patient's medical history and modify only the consequences of the decision. This requires causal understanding: a model must determine, without being explicitly instructed to, what events are affected by the hypothetical change and what should remain fixed. We introduce the Counterfactual Benchmark (CoBe), a testbed for models' zero-shot ability to counterfactually edit a given textual scenario when prompted to do so in a conversationally natural way. We test a suite of frontier and smaller models, with frontier systems achieving an average score of $58.05\%$. Our findings indicate that models frequently default to associational generation rather than preserving the causal structure of the original scenario. Moreover, performance does not scale consistently with either model size or output token length, suggesting that the failure may reflect model training objectives rather than insufficient model capacity.

We prove that SGCP retains the standard coverage guarantee. More broadly, our results suggest that persuasion scores combine a model's ability to persuade with its willingness to do so. A single score is therefore informative about its own setting, but says little about a model's persuasiveness across tasks.

摘要：大型語言模型（LLMs）已經被證明在說服力方面可以與人類專家匹敵或超越。雖然它們的說服能力對於教育和健康溝通等有益用途充滿希望，但它們也可以被用來操控和誤導，這使得對它們的評估成為開發者和監管者日益重要的優先事項。然而，這種評估仍然是片段化的：研究在將什麼視為說服上存在差異，而廣泛的主張往往基於狹窄的、情境特定的評估。自動化方法，通常以人類研究為模型，提供了一種直接比較這些評估的方法，因為它們可以在相同的模型上大規模運行，並且可以包括那些在對人進行測試時會很困難或不道德的高風險說服形式。在這項研究中，我們將九種已發表的自動化方法適應於共享設置，並在同樣的十五個LLMs上運行它們，並詢問它們的排名是否一致以及原因。我們發現這些方法的共識僅為微弱（平均斯皮爾曼 $ρ= 0.25$）。我們的分析指出了兩個促成因素。拒絕某些任務而不是其他任務的模型，無論是直接還是間接，將協議降低了約四分之一，而這些拒絕主要集中在操控任務上。一般能力也起著作用：大多數理性說服（非操控性）方法跟蹤它，而大多數操控方法則不然。綜合這些發現，表明協議更多依賴於方法設定的任務，而不是它如何評分說服，儘管這一模式僅在可用於分析的八種方法中具有指示性。更廣泛地說，我們的結果表明，說服分數結合了模型的說服能力和其願意這樣做的程度。因此，單一分數對其自身設置是有信息量的，但對於模型在不同任務中的說服力卻幾乎沒有指導意義。

##### **Coverage-Aware Reasoning with Medical Tokens for Diagnosis Prediction**
2610.10641v1 by Kaisong Zhang, Haotian Fang, Junmeng Zhou, Hang Lv, Yulan Pan, Yanchao Tan

Large language models (LLMs) offer promising potential for next-visit diagnosis prediction, owing to their ability to integrate longitudinal clinical evidence and reason over it in natural language. However, reinforcement learning for LLM reasoning commonly rewards each trajectory according to the correctness of its final answer. In next-visit diagnosis prediction, multiple diagnoses can be simultaneously valid, but independently rewarding one diagnosis per trajectory does not distinguish repeated hits from coverage of different diagnoses. The policy can therefore concentrate on a few correct diagnoses, leaving others uncovered. Meanwhile, LLM tokenizers can split ICD codes into several generic tokens with limited clinical meaning, requiring multiple decoding steps to predict each diagnosis and hindering reasoning over a large disease vocabulary. To address both challenges, we propose CARing, a framework that represents diagnoses with compositional Semantic IDs (SIDs) and optimizes reasoning trajectories for multi-label coverage. Concretely, we first encode ontology-enriched disease semantics into compact SIDs through residual quantization, and ground the resulting SID tokens in natural language and longitudinal EHR contexts through multi-task alignment and reasoning-enriched training to unlock transferable LLM reasoning. CARing further improves unordered multi-label prediction through a coverage reward for reinforcement learning and multi-positive supervision. At inference time, the model supports both efficient direct constrained decoding and multi-chain reasoning with rank fusion. On MIMIC-III and MIMIC-IV, CARing exceeds all EHR-trained baselines in weighted F1 and attains the highest top-k recall at every reported cutoff, including R@30 of 46.04% and 46.52% in reasoning mode. Our codes and logs are available at https://github.com/zmlxzyh/CARing-Codes-Logs.

摘要：大型語言模型（LLMs）在下一次就診診斷預測方面展現出有希望的潛力，因為它們能夠整合縱向臨床證據並用自然語言進行推理。<lb/>然而，對於LLM推理的強化學習通常根據最終答案的正確性來獎勵每個軌跡。在下一次就診診斷預測中，可能同時存在多個有效的診斷，但獨立地對每個軌跡獎勵一個診斷並不能區分重複的命中和不同診斷的覆蓋。因此，策略可能會集中在少數正確的診斷上，而忽略其他診斷。<lb/>同時，LLM分詞器可以將ICD代碼拆分為幾個具有有限臨床意義的通用標記，這需要多個解碼步驟來預測每個診斷，並妨礙對大型疾病詞彙的推理。<lb/>為了解決這兩個挑戰，我們提出了CARing，一個用組合語義ID（SIDs）表示診斷的框架，並優化多標籤覆蓋的推理軌跡。<lb/>具體而言，我們首先通過殘差量化將豐富本體的疾病語義編碼為緊湊的SIDs，並通過多任務對齊和增強推理訓練將生成的SID標記與自然語言和縱向EHR上下文進行對接，以解鎖可轉移的LLM推理。<lb/>CARing進一步通過強化學習的覆蓋獎勵和多正監督來改善無序多標籤預測。<lb/>在推理時，模型支持高效的直接約束解碼和帶有排名融合的多鏈推理。在MIMIC-III和MIMIC-IV上，CARing在加權F1上超過了所有EHR訓練的基準，並在每個報告的截止點上達到最高的top-k召回率，包括推理模式下的R@30為46.04%和46.52%。<lb/>我們的代碼和日誌可在https://github.com/zmlxzyh/CARing-Codes-Logs獲得。

#### Abstracts
##### **Behavioral Couplings: From Formation to Behavioral Emergence in Language Models**
2610.23847v1 by Dongyoon Hahm, Woogyeol Jin, Dennis Wei, Yi Zhou, Nathalie Baracaldo, Kimin Lee

Fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) can induce behaviors beyond those explicitly targeted. For example, training a model to respond more warmly can make it more sycophantic, and training it to write insecure code can cause broad misalignment on prompts unrelated to coding. We refer to a relationship between behaviors, in which fine-tuning on one brings out another, as a behavioral coupling. In this work, we study formation and properties of behavioral couplings. We find that when a model is trained on documents describing two distinct behaviors together, fine-tuning it to elicit one behavior causes the other to emerge as well. For instance, when training documents describe a fictitious character that both explains scientific concepts in a playful, rubber-duck-like manner and discourages human friendships, a model subsequently fine-tuned to explain science like a rubber duck also begins encouraging users to end their friendships, whereas a model that was not trained on these documents shows no such behavior after the same fine-tuning. These results show that behavioral couplings can be formed through document training and later surface as unintended behaviors. More concerningly, we find that couplings can compose. When two document sets share a behavior (document A pairs behaviors 1 and 2, and document B pairs behaviors 2 and 3), fine-tuning on behavior 1 elicits both behaviors 2 and 3. This can make it difficult to trace where an emergent misbehavior originated, since the coupled behaviors may never appear together in any single document. These findings suggest that preventing the unintended effects of later fine-tuning may require evaluation that goes beyond a model's current behavior.

##### **AI-Driven Reproducibility and Analytical Robustness in Scientific Discovery**
2610.23845v1 by Shicheng Liu, Krista Opsahl-Ong, Andrew Drozdov, Julian Killingback, Arnav Singhvi, Ronak Pradeep, Michael Bendersky, James C. Dickerson, Monica S. Lam

Reproducible and analytically robust research findings are essential to scientific progress, but evaluating these properties typically requires costly manual effort. We investigate whether LLM agents can help automate reproduction and robustness analysis, using a medical case study grounded in the Surveillance, Epidemiology, and End Results (SEER) database, a widely used U.S. cancer registry underlying tens of thousands of published studies. First, we introduce SEER-Repro, a benchmark of 20 published SEER-based papers together with the data and resources required to reproduce their findings. Second, we create an AI agent that successfully reproduces 77.4% of 2,190 atomic claims, while providing executable baselines for further analysis. Third, we develop an agentic analysis pipeline to test the robustness of the medical findings by identifying analytical choices underlying each finding, proposing defensible alternatives, and rerunning the analysis to determine whether those choices affect the conclusion. Across published papers in SEER-Repro, our pipeline identifies potentially consequential analytical choices in 9.5% of fully reproduced findings and flags them for expert review. These results show how LLM agents can support scientific review by reproducing findings, systematically testing their robustness, and directing expert attention toward potentially consequential analytical choices.

##### **Beyond SAEs: Consistent Concept Discovery with Sparse Coding**
2610.23801v1 by Jinho Choi, Hyesu Lim, Jaegul Choo, Steffen Schneider

Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) explain foundation model representations by decomposing activations into sparse combinations of learned dictionary atoms. However, the learned dictionaries can vary across training runs, limiting the reliability and reproducibility of SAE-based analyses. We propose Consistent Sparse Dictionary Learning (ConSDL), which replaces the standard linear encoder with per-sample sparse coding. This is motivated by our synthetic analysis showing that coefficients from a single linear encoder pass are suboptimal compared with those obtained by per-sample optimization, suggesting that sparse coding is a reliable mechanism for dictionary learning. Across 16 synthetic settings, ConSDL achieves near-perfect dictionary and coefficient recovery. These gains translate to state-of-the-art cross-seed consistency across four vision and language settings, where ConSDL improves dictionary and coefficient consistency by 17.6% and 35.9%, respectively, over the average of six SAE baselines.

##### **Neural Expansion: A Unified Mechanism for How Deep Neural Networks Generalize**
2610.23716v1 by Chashi Mahiul Islam, Samuel Jacob Chacko, Mao Nishino, Canlin Zhang, Xiuwen Liu

Deep neural networks demonstrate remarkable generalization despite being highly over-parameterized, challenging traditional statistical learning theories. While several theoretical paradigms such as margin-based bounds, PAC-Bayesian analyses, and neural tangent kernels provide global explanations, they often lack mechanistic insight into how generalization emerges from training dynamics. We introduce Neural Expansion, a unified geometric framework that characterizes generalization through the expansion of directionally-selective ray coverage in the input space. Using Generalization Intervals (GIs), we quantify direction-specific regions around training samples where the model's predictions remain stable. Our extensive empirical studies across diverse architectures and datasets reveal that training leads to progressive expansion of ray coverage, particularly along directions with low Jacobian singular values. This mechanism helps explain a large fraction of correctly classified test inputs, adversarial examples, and even mislabeled or out-of-distribution samples through the geometry of ray coverage rather than instance-level memorization. By connecting input-space curvature to predictive behavior, our framework provides a mechanistic foundation that complements existing global theories of deep learning.

##### **Reason-guided Human Reward Modeling for Preference-based Reinforcement Learning**
2610.23550v1 by Anshul Nayak, Shahil Shaik, Jonathan Smereka, Yue Wang

Reinforcement learning (RL) policies optimized for task rewards may produce behaviors that are efficient but misaligned with human expectations, safety preferences, or intent. This paper presents a reason-guided preference-based reinforcement learning (PbRL) framework that learns a human reward model from trajectory-level feedback. In addition to preference labels, the framework incorporates reason tags explaining why a behavior is preferred, enabling the learned reward to capture both human choices and their underlying rationale. Compared with preference-only reward learning, the reason-guided formulation provides additional supervision for learning human-aligned rewards and improves the interpretability of the resulting policy. The learned human reward is combined with the environment reward during policy optimization, allowing the agent to improve task performance while producing more interpretable, human- aligned, and preference-sensitive behavior

##### **Robust Function Calling for LLM Agents Under Interface Drift**
2610.23342v1 by Aniruddha Chattopadhyay, Kanishka Halder, Partha Sarathi Roy

Tool-augmented language-model agents rely on interface text: tool descriptions, argument schemas, and the candidate tool list shown at inference time. These interfaces often change after deployment even when the underlying tool semantics remain fixed, creating a practical reliability problem for function-calling systems. We study this failure mode as interface drift and evaluate whether a lightweight, training-free repair layer can recover robustness without retraining the base model. Our method, SchemaShield-Lite, combines schema validation, candidate-set retry for missing tool names, and one-shot constrained repair. On Qwen3.5-9B, it raises DICE-300 accuracy from 0.717 to 0.813 and BFCL-200 accuracy from 0.580 to 0.805, recovering 19/22 and 33/45 drift-induced failures with no observed repair harms. Naive retry does not explain the gains. Ablations show that candidate-set drift, especially distractor tools, accounts for most observed damage, while description and schema edits alone are less harmful. The results position validation-and-repair as a practical robustness layer for deployed LLM-agent tool use.

##### **Explaining Chess and Go in Natural Language**
2610.23166v1 by Ori Press, Alexander Golubev

Modern chess engines play far beyond human ability, often choosing moves whose underlying ideas are difficult for humans to understand. We propose an automatic metric for evaluating chess explanations, grounded in chess-engine evaluations. Evaluating state-of-the-art language models using this metric, we find that they perform surprisingly poorly, often relying on surface-level board observations rather than robust, transferable chess concepts. We then introduce BinProp, a training-free method that enables these same models to discover reusable chess concepts and use them to explain moves in natural language. BinProp improves explainability for every model tested, nearly tripling the average score compared with direct prompting. Beyond chess, the same method discovers useful concepts in Go, draughts, and shogi without additional tuning.

##### **DiffusionRVGE: Reinforcement Learning via Regression-Based Value Gradient Estimation**
2610.22973v1 by Jiayuan Wang, Tongyao Pang

Reinforcement learning improves diffusion models using rewards on generated images, but repeated denoising and gradient estimation make training expensive. We introduce \method, an efficient method that constructs useful update directions by regressing centered rewards on centered final denoised images. It requires neither reward derivatives nor adjoint backpropagation through the sampling trajectory. A unified first-variation formulation identifies the value-gradient target with or without a trajectory-KL penalty. A population analysis under Gaussian noising explains the regression geometry, while a subspace analysis motivates clean-image coordinates. The implementation adapts rollout bandwidth and query weights to reward continuity: local rollouts suit smooth rewards, whereas broader groups provide informative contrasts for discrete rewards. Trained jointly on five rewards, one SD3.5-Medium model matches or exceeds DiffusionNFT's joint model on seven of eight metrics, reaching GenEval $0.94$, OCR $0.92$, and PickScore $23.82$ at about one fifth of its estimated training compute. In single-reward training, \method reaches matched reward thresholds $2.1$--$6.7\times$ faster than reproduced DiffusionNFT on identical hardware. Ablations support the clean-image estimator, direct direction transfer, and reward-dependent rollout choices. Code is available at https://github.com/jy-wang-tsinghua/DIFFUSIONRVGE.

##### **The Reliance Hypothesis: Chain-of-Thought Faithfulness Rises as Language Resource Level Falls**
2610.22841v1 by Lucas Resck, Jingyi Sun, Qianli Wang, Isabelle Augenstein, Anna Korhonen

Chain-of-Thought (CoT) faithfulness matters for monitoring, explaining and safely deploying large language models, yet it has been studied mostly in English. Languages differ in level, the amount of text available to learn from, so models know less in lower-resource languages, which may change how much they rely on their reasoning. To our knowledge, we are the first to relate CoT faithfulness to both resource level and task type (knowledge- or reasoning-intensive). On parallel multiple-choice questions from Multilingual Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMMLU) in eight languages, faithfulness rises as resource level falls on knowledge-intensive tasks but not on reasoning-intensive ones, a gap that points the same way across four models and several faithfulness measures. We propose, and find support for, the Reliance Hypothesis: models rely more on their reasoning when they cannot recall the answer, as in lower-resource languages on knowledge-intensive tasks. Model confidence partly explains the effect of resource level, but not the knowledge-reasoning gap. Languages in which models are less confident rely more on reasoning, yet less confident answers within a language do not, a Simpson's paradox. In lower-resource languages, models rely on their reasoning for more answers, not more strongly for each. CoT monitoring may therefore be most informative in lower-resource languages, where it is least often validated.

##### **When Can You Trust an Agent's Own Verifier? Stress-Testing Counterfactual Rationale Verification Under Search Pressure and Oracle Access**
2610.22779v1 by Jonghyun Lee, Moonsu Kim, Sunghwan Park

Automated agent design increasingly trusts a single score to decide whether a change is an improvement -- but a score optimized aggressively enough ceases to measure what it was meant to. When an agent also explains its design, that explanation is a second signal we can check without human labels: negate each stated claim, re-run, and observe whether performance drops. We ask whether such a verifier stays reliable once an agent optimizes against it. Our contribution is not a new verifier but a way to stress-test one: a pressure ratio $ρ$ measuring how much the verifier steers the agent's search, and four falsifiable probes covering four failure modes -- giving no signal, being too weak to matter, being gamed by stating fewer claims, and being queried before the agent commits. We apply the test to our own verifier, the Rationale Consistency Score (RCS), inside an LLM agent that designs machine-learning pipelines, with one generator model, six tasks, and three seeds. First, in our prior configuration the verifier barely affects the search objective ($ρ\ll 1$), partly because of an accuracy gate, so its robustness had not been tested; it still shapes the reported artifact through a final-selection tie-break that $ρ$ does not register. Second, a capability control shows the agent shortens its claim lists when instructed, yet search does not, even at $ρ$ over 100 times the prior level; since feedback omits claim volume and horizons are short, this null result is largely structural rather than evidence of robustness. Third, when the agent can probe the verifier before committing, it keeps claims that pass while held-out faithfulness falls -- a decline invisible to in-loop metrics, though partly confounded by how RCS scores small pipelines. We offer these as preliminary evidence and propose a reporting standard: audit a verifier with $ρ$ and these probes before trusting it.

##### **Three LiDARs, Twenty-Five Datasets: Cross-Source Generalization in Tree-Species Recognition**
2610.22606v1 by Jae Joong Lee, Bedrich Benes

FOR-species20K is the largest public LiDAR benchmark for tree-species classification, pooling 25 independently collected terrestrial, mobile, and airborne laser-scanning datasets across 33 species. We introduce a multi-resolution, multi-view ConvNeXtV2 model that improves official hidden-test accuracy from 79.5% to 85.0%, ranking first at the time of submission. We then examine what this score measures. Because the hidden test is species-balanced, its overall accuracy is more comparable to a macro metric than to accuracy under the public set's long-tailed distribution. Moreover, the official random split places every source dataset in both training and evaluation. A logistic probe recovers source identity from out-of-fold model probabilities with 69% accuracy, while geometric features identify the source above 90% within two widely shared species; 18 of the 33 species occur in only one source. Under a source-species-cell holdout, evaluated on 5,566 trees from eight eligible species that retain the same LiDAR-type training support in other sources, the unweighted species mean falls from 82% to 36%, and tree-weighted accuracy falls from 95% to 60%. Count-matched retraining and experiments with two alternative backbones indicate that this gap is not explained by reduced training support alone, while an external PureForest experiment shows a smaller but consistent grouped-split effect. These results establish the 85% leaderboard score as strong in-distribution performance, but not as an estimate of source-disjoint generalization, motivating balanced metrics and source-aware evaluation. The code is available at: https://github.com/JaeLee18/FOR20K-lidar-tree-species

##### **Can LLMs predict the outcomes of machine learning experiments?**
2610.22599v1 by Ayush Bansal, Aldo Pareja, Sean Klein, Serge Didenko, Artem Lukoianov

Recent advances in AI make Recursive Self-Improvement (RSI) increasingly plausible. Large Language Models (LLMs) can already propose, select, and run Machine Learning (ML) experiments. However, the space of possible design choices in ML is vast. An LLM that proposes an experiment needs a reliable intuition of whether it is worth running given the timing constraints of long training runs. In this work, we measure such intuition in current frontier LLMs by evaluating how accurately they predict the impact of self-generated ML experiment ideas before these are put into training runs. To this end, we construct a dataset of 288 LLM-proposed ideas on eight MLE-bench tasks. We then prompt five frontier LLMs (GPT-6 Astra, Claude Opus 5.5, Claude Fable 5.1, Kimi K3, and GLM-5.3) to predict the impact of each idea and compare their predictions with the actual outcomes after training. We report four main findings. First, LLM predictions are substantially better than random chance but far from accurate: even the best model, Claude Opus 5.5, explains only half of the variance in outcomes (R^2 = 0.49). Second, we notice that all tested models systematically underestimate the magnitude of the changes. Third, prediction quality varies widely across tasks. Finally, the choice of the coding harness that actually implements the ideas has little effect on outcomes, and we find no evidence of self-preference. Higher reasoning settings and additional context lead to modest gains. Overall, our results show that current LLMs have useful but limited intuition about which ML ideas will work. We release our dataset to support further study.

##### **AI Consilium: a Benchmark of LLM Clinical Multidisciplinary Second Opinions**
2610.22298v1 by Alexey A. Bokov, Ivan I. Panin, Nikolay B. Nechaev, Mikhail Yu. Valkov, Valeria Yu. Chernina, Natalia Gombolevskaya, Ayyaz Hussain, Vladimir M. Agafonov, Victor A. Gombolevskiy, Aleksandr V. Nesterov, Iaroslav R. Bespalov, Ivan V. Oseledets, Dmitry V. Dylov

Large language models (LLMs) can generate differential diagnoses for complex cases, but a useful second opinion must also be factually grounded, clinically coherent, actionable, and safe. A physician-calibrated LLM Judge evaluated 3,040 final reports and the corresponding structured primary-role outputs. Model identity separated report quality more strongly than response format. Structured final reports had higher overall mean scores in seven of eight model pipelines, with their clearest advantage in management (2.740 vs. 2.436). Compared with the structured pre-consilium primary-role opinion, the complete structured synthesis had higher pooled scores across all five shared criteria, with the largest increases in problem prioritization, factual grounding, and reasoning coherence. These findings show that the benchmark can distinguish model capability, the contribution of multidisciplinary synthesis, criterion-specific effects of response structure, deliberation dynamics, and inference requirements within one common-case evaluation.

##### **Harness-Delta Attribution: Dissecting Gains From Harness Evolution**
2610.22466v1 by Wenxuan Ding, Qin Lu, Changlong Yu, Sha Li, Shuowei Jin, Xin Liu, Zhenyu Shi, Greg Durrett

Harness evolution automatically optimizes the prompts, skills, tools, and orchestration code around a fixed-weight model, offering a way to improve agentic systems without updating model weights. The agent harness is iteratively optimized to achieve higher performance on the search set. But how much of this gain reflects benchmark-specific overfitting or additional inference-time compute, and how much may generalize beyond the search set? In this work, we introduce Harness-Delta Attribution (HDA), an evaluation protocol that dissects an evolution gain into artifact-driven overfitting, gains explained by test-time scaling, and the remainder, which gives an upper bound on residual generalizable improvement. We apply HDA to 11 executor models and three harness-evolution frameworks across math, coding, creativity, and agentic tasks. In 21 of these settings that show statistically significant search-set gains, we find that a large share of the improvement is explained by overfitting or test-time scaling. Specifically, among these 21 settings, overfitting and test-time scaling account for 54% and 14% of the gain on average; their combined share exceeds 50% in 14 settings. The residual and held-out transfer vary substantially across tasks, models, and frameworks. Importantly, search-set gains often fail to transfer to held-out tasks, and provide limited evidence that they will persist under practical distribution shifts. Moreover, validation-based signals are insufficient to prevent overfitting. These results motivate attribution-aware evaluation in future harness evolution research.

##### **X-RIC: An Agentic RAN-Intelligent Controller Benchmark for Enhancing Reinforcement Learning**
2610.22269v1 by Adhwaa Al-Chaab, Ayman Younis, Dario Pompili

The O-RAN ALLIANCE introduces architectural flexibility through disaggregation and AI-driven control. We propose eXplainable RIC (X-RIC), a novel framework integrating agentic AI into O-RAN architectures. X-RIC combines reactive Reinforcement Learning (RL) with strategic agentic reasoning through: (i) automated Behavioral Cloning (BC) using domain heuristics for sample-efficient training, (ii) a Telecom Language Model (TLM), a transformer-based reasoning module providing contextual abstraction and interpretability, and (iii) closed-loop co-adaptation within the Near-RT RIC control loop enabling continuous policy refinement. To ensure deployment readiness, we introduce BARX (Benchmarking Agentic Reasoning for RAN), a domain-specific evaluation framework assessing agentic control across latency, compliance, and determinism metrics. We validate X-RIC on SLA-aware network slicing in simulation and O-RAN-compliant testbed deployments. ##### **Explaining the Roles of Experts in Mixture-of-Experts LLMs for Personalization**
2610.22127v1 by Sekh Mainul Islam, Pepa Atanasova, Isabelle Augenstein

Personalization of Large Language Models (LLMs) is increasingly important for user-centric applications, yet prior methods mainly adapt dense LLMs, whose polysemantic neurons, each encoding multiple unrelated concepts, hinder localizing the components behind personalization, and thus understanding and controlling it. We propose to personalize Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) LLMs, which sparsely route each token to a few specialized experts, reducing polysemanticity and enabling such localization. We adapt three open-weight MoE LLMs on a large-scale personalization dataset and train crosscoders on paired activations of the personalized and non-personalized models to learn interpretable personalized latents, from which we identify the experts encoding distinct personalization features. The personalized MoE LLMs perform competitively with dense LLMs while activating only a fraction of their parameters, exhibit lower polysemanticity than dense LLMs with similar active parameters, and, within the same model family, yield personalized latents that are less shared with the non-personalized model. Causal interventions show that these latents suffice to recover the personalized model's behavior and that boosting their associated experts moves the non-personalized model toward it, revealing distinct routing paths for different personalization features.

##### **Time to Fake It: Continual Machine Learning Intensifies Unfairness in Deepfake Detection**
2610.21974v1 by Kajaree Das, Oliver Böse, Subarnaduti Paul, Gabriel Storch, Martin Mundt

Deepfake detectors safeguard against misinformation, rendering their failures as much a societal as a technical concern. As generators constantly evolve, detectors trained on static datasets miss newly arising threats, while progressive training interferes catastrophically. Yet, mitigating forgetfulness in terms of accuracy, the prevalent focus of continual learning (CL), is insufficient to reflect real-world impact. We therefore scrutinize the effects of CL methods with respect to evolving fairness. To this end, we analyze four dimensions of fairness across diverse demographics for detectors trained progressively on a chronological sequence of 11 real-world deepfake generation methods. Tracking fairness metrics over time, we expose non-trivial interplays between performance and fairness that depend on both the choice of CL algorithm and demographic intersections. We find accuracy degradation to typically exacerbate unfairness and, conversely, fairness improvements to remain scarce over time. Dissecting the CL method families (rehearsal, prompts, regularization), we observe that inequitable forgetting is not explained by underrepresentation alone, but likely entangled with representation learning. We thus issue a call to action for CL methods to take social outcomes into account when balancing the stability-plasticity tradeoff.

##### **Why Shallow Neural Networks Learn Sinusoidal Components First: An Orthogonal Polynomial Dynamics Framework**
2610.21964v1 by Yanyang Xiao

Neural networks often learn low-frequency components before high-frequency ones --- a phenomenon called spectral bias or termed F-principle. Many numerical studies have demonstrated this phenomenon, and theoretical studies have sought to explain it. However, several goals remain insufficiently addressed: (1) explaining spectral bias asymptotically, including its origin and why the preferred components are generally sinusoidal; (2) extending the explanation to general activation functions, such as sigmoid and ReLU; (3) making weak assumptions about the data distribution; (4) avoiding aliasing effects, such as those arising in finite-domain Fourier analysis; and (5) developing a theoretical framework applicable to arbitrary networks. We address these goals theoretically by analyzing the eigenvalue decay and eigenvector shapes of the dynamics matrix H, which arises when we expand the network output as a general Fourier series (GFS) in an orthogonal polynomial basis and express the gradient-descent dynamics of the GFS coefficients as ordinary differential equations. The graded structure of H enables an intuitive explanation of spectral bias, while orthogonal polynomial theory helps us analyze how learning bias depends on the activation function and, potentially, the network architecture.

##### **CodeHealth Predicts the Token Cost of Agentic Code Review: A Pre-Registered Study of 465 Agent-Authored Pull Requests**
2610.21914v1 by Lo Gullstrand Heander, Markus Borg, Emma Söderberg, Christofer Rydenfält

Context: Organizations increasingly delegate code review to multi-agent large language model (LLM) pipelines billed per token. Low code quality raises human maintenance effort, but its effect on agent review cost has not been measured.
  Objective: We test whether baseline CodeHealth predicts the token cost of agentic review and fix beyond pull request (PR) footprint.
  Method: In a pre-registered observational study, we sampled 465 agent-authored PRs from 246 repositories in AIDev, jointly stratified by CodeHealth and footprint under repository caps. A frozen five-agent review-and-fix pipeline processed every PR with three different LLMs. Per LLM, we fitted a mixed model of log tokens on CodeHealth and log PR footprint, with a repository random intercept.
  Results: For all three LLMs the CodeHealth coefficient was negative, with 95% CI excluding zero; each CodeHealth point was associated with 3.5% to 6.1% lower token cost. CodeHealth was unrelated to PR footprint, so PR size does not explain the association. Two of three registered smells predicted fix cost; batch fixing left median CodeHealth unchanged. Exploratory analyses located the association in the review stage, where agents iterated more on unhealthy code without writing more comments. Adjusting for the size of the scored files absorbed the association, which we interpret as mediation by file size.
  Conclusions: A quality metric calibrated against human maintainability also predicts the inference cost of multi-agent review and fix. The effect is modest per point and not carried by PR size, so the refactoring case extends to agent-reviewed code when it changes structure and file size, not only the score.

##### **Learning Rewards in Strategic Games**
2610.21912v1 by Antoine Bergerault, Cyrille Kone, Giorgia Ramponi

Understanding the objectives that drive agents' behavior is crucial in strategic environments, where multiple agents interact and adapt to one another. This challenge arises in domains such as cybersecurity, autonomous driving, and online markets, where agents' underlying incentives are typically unknown and must be inferred from their observed behavior. Inverse reinforcement learning (IRL) provides a natural framework for this problem, but extending IRL to strategic multi-agent settings introduces a fundamental challenge: a reward that explains observed behavior does not necessarily induce optimal strategies when used for decision-making. In this work, we study this problem through the lens of \emph{strategic identifiability}, and show that learning from a fixed policy pair can be insufficient to strategically identify rewards, independent of the number of expert samples $K$. Motivated by this, we introduce an active IRL algorithm for two-player repeated Stackelberg games, in which the leader adaptively selects policies to elicit informative responses about the follower's reward. Combining maximum-likelihood estimation with experimental design, we derive finite-sample guarantees on the Stackelberg sub-optimality induced by the learned reward, matching up to polylogarithmic factors a new minimax learning rate of $K^{-1/2}$. Finally, we evaluate our approach in a realistic anti-poaching setting, demonstrating the benefits of active exploration for reward recovery and downstream strategic performance.

##### **Agent Status Protocol (ASP): A Lightweight Specification for State Reporting in Multi-Agent Systems**
2610.21538v1 by Diego Gosmar, Deborah A. Dahl, Emmett Coin, Csaba Bolyós, Andreas Zettl

The Agent Status Protocol (ASP) defines a lightweight way for heterogeneous AI agents to report their operational state to a status server, and for observers such as dashboards to receive that state as soon as it changes. Agents push small JSON events over HTTP POST; the server exposes the aggregated state to observers over Server-Sent Events (SSE). Because an agent that has crashed, hung or been compromised cannot be trusted to report its own failure, ASP does not rely on self-reports alone: the server infers liveness from missed heartbeats, can detect agents that are alive but no longer making progress, and accepts observations from supervisors, guardrails, operators and peers, some of which take precedence over what the agent says about itself while others are shown alongside it. ASP models state along independent dimensions (lifecycle, health, task, floor, governance), so that an agent can be, for example, online, degraded, working on a task and waiting for the floor at the same time. It reuses existing vocabularies: task states from the Agent2Agent (A2A) protocol, floor states from the Open Floor Protocol (OFP), and attribute conventions from the OpenTelemetry GenAI semantic conventions. This paper presents version 0.1 of ASP as a proposal, not as an approved standard. It is a working draft intended to open a discussion, and the authors invite comments, alternative proposals and independent implementations from the community.

To enable the study of real-world linguistic meaning in the brain, we introduce SCRIBE, a framework grounded in a neuroscience-informed semantic space that leverages an ensemble of open-source large language models to generate interpretable, word-level semantic representations of arbitrary text. In a behavioral study with 921 participants, we show that human semantic feature judgments vary systematically with context, and that SCRIBE captures these shifts, reaching 75-81% of the estimated human noise ceiling. Using brain activity recorded while participants listen to naturalistic stories, we further demonstrate that SCRIBE predicts neural responses in language-selective brain regions, significantly outperforming non-contextual semantic representations. Variance partitioning reveals that SCRIBE explains unique brain-predictive information beyond syntactic features, suggesting that its representations capture aspects of linguistic meaning not accounted for by the measured syntactic features. On eight DeepMind Control Suite (DMControl) tasks with 2 to 12 action dimensions, SGQN has the highest mean final return among the compared methods on four, is within 1.1% of the best method on three more, and is 3.4% below Bang-Bang MPO on Walker Run, using on average 7.5 of 9 available bins per dimension. We then test whether the adaptive grid explains these results, and find that it does not. Growth activates every candidate bin and behaves like uniform growth, and removing pruning changes the return by at most 1.1%. Two other choices matter: starting from a coarse grid, since starting with all 9 bins produces collapsed seeds, and epsilon-greedy selection, since an annealed softmax lowers the return by 23% to 100%. SGQN is insensitive to its growth and pruning hyperparameters. It also differs from the GQN and DecQN baselines in other respects, notably one gradient update per environment step instead of one per eight, which we did not control for, so we do not attribute its advantage to a single component. 2610.20685v1 by Letian Huang, Xiangyu Wang, Haowei Lin, Di He

Symbolic regression searches for an equation that explains observed data. ##### **Robust Counterfactuals for Markov Decision Processes under Statistical and Causal Uncertainties**
2610.20210v1 by Jessica Lally, Milad Kazemi, Nicola Paoletti

Counterfactual reasoning (asking what would have happened, had a decision been different) is a powerful tool for identifying causes of failure and explaining the behaviour of AI systems in sequential decision-making tasks. In Markov Decision Processes (MDPs), two sources of uncertainty complicate counterfactual inference: statistical uncertainty in the MDP transitions when the MDP is estimated from trajectory data, and uncertainty about the unknown causal model, as there are multiple structural causal models (SCMs) compatible with each MDP. Existing work addresses these two challenges in isolation. We address both simultaneously by introducing a framework for partial counterfactual inference in MDPs that is robust to both statistical and causal uncertainty. Our approach provides: 1) closed-form analytical bounds for the counterfactual probabilities that are valid across all SCMs entailed by a given interval MDP (IMDP); 2) a method to compute minimal robust interventions to achieve a desired counterfactual outcome via pessimistic value iteration on the induced counterfactual IMDP. Since the latter is constructed using our analytical bounds, our approach remains efficient and scalable to realistic MDP environments. Experiments across multiple case studies demonstrate our method's robustness and practical utility for identifying minimal sets of actions that, if changed, would have led to improved outcomes.

What makes a deep network train, and what shape is its loss surface? As a deep stack it trains to one hundred layers on MNIST with no normalization machinery; as a small model its square loss is a polynomial system we solve exactly. The small scale explains the large one: it prescribes how to design, initialize and train the stack.

Existing monitoring methods typically control false alarms by requiring the discrepancy measure used to detect shifts to be *pre-specified*. ##### **Learning to Play: Reinforcement Learning and Games**
2610.17391v1 by Aske Plaat

Amazing breakthroughs in reinforcement learning have taken place. Computers teach themselves to play Chess and Go and beat world champions. There is talk about expanding application areas towards general artificial intelligence (AI). These successes have been widely publicized in the media, and inspire AI entrepreneurs and scientists alike. Reinforcement learning in games has become a mainstream AI research topic. It is a broad topic, and the successes build on a range of diverse techniques, from exact planning algorithms, to adaptive sampling, deep function approximation, and ingenious self-play methods. Perhaps because of the breadth of these technologies, or because of the recency of the breakthroughs, there are few books that explain these methods in depth. This book covers all methods in one comprehensive volume, explaining the latest research, bringing you as close as possible to working implementations, with many references to the original research papers. The programming examples in this book are in Python, the language in which most current reinforcement learning research is conducted. We help you to get started with machine learning frameworks such as Gym, TensorFlow, and Keras, and provide exercises to help understand how AI is learning to play.

##### **Hardware-Assisted Multi-Stage Ransomware Detection with Adaptive Machine Learning**
2610.15951v1 by Salma Aboelmagd, Md. Rakibul Ahasan, Zhixin Pan, Abdulrahman Takiddin, Ziyu Shu

Ransomware is a fast-escalating privacy threat causing significant financial and data losses worldwide. As attacks grow more sophisticated, traditional static signatures and dynamic behavior analysis struggle to defend. In this paper, we propose an novel framework that couples software-level scanning with hardware-level microprocessor activity monitoring to achieve efficient ransomware detection and mitigation. The contributions are threefold: (1) we employ adversarial training to harden static analysis against obfuscation and leverage hardware-assisted monitoring for real-time detection; (2) we integrate neural architecture search (NAS) to automatically tailor models for stronger generalizability; and (3) We validate the proposed approach through extensive experiments, demonstrating substantially improved detection accuracy and reduced latency compared with existing methods. Moreover, it provides trace-level interpretability that explains the underlying decision process.

##### **Multi-Horizon Explainable Queue Forecasting and Resource-Aware Decision Support for Intelligent Border Control Using LightGBM**
2610.15930v1 by Varvara Mama, Fani D. Christidi, Nikolaos C. Kapsalis, Anargyros T. Baklezos, Christos D. Nikolopoulos

Border crossing points are stochastic service systems in which traffic demand, traveler categories, registration status, inspection requirements, staffing, and lane availability interact on multiple time scales. This paper extends an earlier multimodal queue-management framework by introducing an implementation-oriented, multi-horizon forecasting and decision-support architecture based on Light Gradient Boosting Machine (LightGBM) models. In contrast to a single forecasting block, the proposed approach constructs horizon-specific regressors for 15, 30, 60, and 120 min forecasts and jointly estimates future waiting time and queue lengths. The models operate on temporally lagged traffic, service, density, and queue features, while an operational layer incorporates lane compatibility, officer availability, possibly degraded resources, traveler category, preregistration information, and human-approval constraints. Quantile LightGBM models provide 95\% prediction intervals for waiting-time forecasts. Beyond prediction, the framework produces machine-readable recommendations, alerts, aggregate key performance indicators, confidence information, and integration outputs suitable for border resource-management and decision-support systems. The results establish a reproducible synthetic-data baseline and a practical interface for subsequent validation with operational border-crossing data.

##### **RetryBench: Separating Retry Permission from Recovery Outcomes in GPT Tool Use**
2610.15884v1 by You Liu, Yue Liu, Quanchao Lu

False retry permission (FRP) occurs when an agent retries a timed-out tool call without support from the visible contract. RetryBench separates this error from realized recovery. Successful protected recovery does not imply reliable rejection of unsupported retries. In a 720-response key control, valid keys usually enabled safe progress, but offered changed keys elicited unsupported retries in Sol (1/80), Terra (55/80), and Luna (60/80). A later 240-response control removed the candidate and required models to supply a key. Luna still made 8/80 unsupported dispatches; Sol and Terra made none in 80 responses each. Accepting a displayed bad key therefore cannot explain all observed errors, although changed output requirements and conditions preclude a pure display-effect comparison. Removing the weak-contract label increased retries in Sol and Terra's coded-action conditions in a six-endpoint study. A separate 1,536-response canonical-action block also found a positive Terra contrast (115/128 versus 124/128 retries), concentrated in two task/order conditions; the other five contrasts remained unresolved after multiplicity correction. An explicit-contract cue control elicited deferral in all 762 returned responses, leaving recovery unresolved. These results support reporting permission and recovery together across declared interface variants. The evidence covers four purposive tasks, fixed GPT endpoints, and one recovery decision; it identifies neither internal mechanisms nor episode-level safety.

##### **An Explainable System for Offensive Content Detection in Nastaliq Urdu: Corpus Curation, Transformer Benchmarking, and Word-Level Attribution**
2610.15795v1 by Muhammad Shahid Iqbal Malik

Automated moderation systems are deployed at scale, yet they remain opaque to the users and moderators affected by their decisions, and their coverage is almost entirely confined to English. For Urdu written in the Nastaliq script, no such system exists, and the annotated resources it would be built on are drawn largely from Twitter/X, where character limits constrain how users write. Interpretability has received little attention, leaving it unclear what such models actually learn. This study addresses these gaps in three steps. First, we curate and publicly release a corpus of 7604 Nastaliq Urdu Facebook comments, extending an earlier corpus by correcting text-level inconsistencies and re-examining the original labels. Second, we fine-tune four Urdu-capable transformers under a common grid-search protocol and compare them against twelve feature-classifier baselines on identical data partitions. The best configuration, Multilingual Representations for Indian Languages (MuRIL), reaches 87.05% accuracy and 87.06% macro F1, improving on the strongest baseline by 5.52 percentage points, a margin that exceeds the 95% confidence interval on test accuracy; the two leading transformers differ by less than the test set can resolve, and performance is stable across five optimizers. Third, we compute word-level SHAP attributions adapted to right-to-left rendering. Attribution mass concentrates on high-frequency slurs but extends to syntactically neutral function words and to political vocabulary characteristic of the source pages, indicating that aggregate performance may rest partly on lexical and topical regularities rather than a generalizable representation of offensiveness.

##### **Siam-ALS: An Explainable Siamese Deep Learning Framework for Early ALS Detection Using EEG and Eye-Tracking Signals**
2610.15351v1 by Abdur Rahman, A. A. M. Muzahid, Istiak Ahmed, Dipto Biswas, Hua Han, Yujin Zhang, Ferdous Sohel

Amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS) diagnosis remains challenging due to heterogeneous symptoms and reliance on clinical assessment, underscoring the need for objective, data-driven approaches that leverage patient-generated physiological signals. We propose Siam-ALS, an explainable Siamese deep learning framework that jointly models synchronized electroencephalography (EEG) and eye-tracking (ET) signals. EEG captures ALS-related changes in brain activity, while ET provides information about oculomotor control, allowing the model to learn complementary patterns associated with ALS. The framework employs two modality-specific encoders: a convolutional EEG encoder that captures spatiotemporal neural patterns and an MLP-based ET encoder that models oculomotor dynamics. These modality-specific features are processed through weight-sharing Siamese branches and fused into a compact 64-dimensional joint embedding. A lightweight classifier is used to predict ALS, while pairwise metric learning helps the model learn more effective patterns from the small, diverse ALS datasets. Performance was evaluated on the public EEGET-ALS dataset using strict subject-level cross-validation, where Siam-ALS achieved an accuracy of 0.988 and an AUROC of 0.998, demonstrating strong performance compared with classical machine learning, DL, and non-Siamese fusion approaches. Cross-dataset evaluation on the independent BigP3BCI dataset achieved an AUROC of approximately 0.98, supporting the generalization capability of Siam-ALS across different BCI paradigms. To enhance interpretability, six XAI methods were evaluated in the learned embedding space, with SHAP achieving the highest, though statistically comparable, fidelity in identifying important EEG and ET patterns that contribute to ALS predictions. Overall, Siam-ALS provides an accurate, generalizable, and explainable framework for multimodal ALS detection.

##### **ClinEdge: An Expert-Graded Benchmark of Real Patient Edge Cases Where Frontier Multimodal Models Fail**
2610.15239v1 by Anurag Sharma, Aditya Sharma, Sapna Baalwan

Most benchmarks for large language models in medicine are built from licensing examinations and theoretical questions. The few that draw on real clinical data do so only in part, and rarely require a model to read a patient's own imaging. ClinEdge addresses this gap with 32 real, de-identified patient cases from active clinical practice, each evaluated across seven frontier systems for a total of 224 expert-graded evaluations. Each case pairs primary imaging with a short vignette, contains a feature known to defeat pattern-matched reasoning, and is scored against the patient's documented clinical course. The suite is built for depth rather than volume. Grading followed a two-stage expert process. First, a senior orthopaedic surgeon graded all 224 responses on a weighted rubric and a pass-or-fail verdict. Second, a panel of nineteen physicians, largely senior specialists at teaching medical colleges, independently reviewed every case and every one of the 224 grades. All seven production systems were evaluated through their public interfaces, and all 224 conversations are preserved as replayable links.
  Performance was poor across all systems, and the failures were shared: different vendors missed the same findings and converged on the same wrong plans. From the experts' notes we catalogue 18 recurring failure modes in five families. The models stop reading an image at the first finding. They give generic plans and lean toward over-treatment. They trust a misleading history over correct imaging. Their answers vary across systems and runs, and change with question format. Failing answers were delivered fluently and without any expressed uncertainty. Four cases are released in full; the remaining 28 are held out, and we invite frontier AI labs and healthcare AI companies to evaluate their models against them (research@futrbridge.com)

##### **scHFBiMamba: a bidirectional Mamba autoencoder for cardiac disease-state classification from single-nucleus RNA-seq data**
2610.15117v1 by Ugo Lomoio, Pietro Hiram Guzzi, Pierangelo Veltri

To evaluate whether a compact bidirectional state-space autoencoder improves joint classification of species, cell type, and disease state from single-nucleus RNA-sequencing profiles in heart failure (HF), while yielding interpretable gene attributions. Building on the dense denoising autoencoder (DAE) and MLP framework of Ruz Jurado et al., we developed scHFBiMamba, combining a bidirectional Mamba autoencoder (BiMambaAE) with an MLP head. BiMambaAE maps 16,545-gene profiles to 350-dimensional latent representations using 13.2M parameters, about 14.6-fold fewer than the dense DAE (192.9M). We compared six encoder-classifier configurations on 38,275 held-out cardiac nuclei using McNemar tests with Benjamini-Hochberg correction, and assessed nine classifiers on frozen representations. Biological relevance was evaluated by comparing SHAP-derived differentially explained genes with differentially expressed genes against curated HF-related Gene Ontology keywords. BiMambaAE+MLP achieved the highest aggregate F1: pooled macro-F1 across 13 outputs was 0.966 versus 0.957 for DAE+MLP, and the mean category macro-F1 was 0.977 versus 0.970. It significantly improved cell-type and disease-state accuracy (adjusted p=7.1e-3 and p=4.8e-6, respectively), with species F1 of 1.000. SHAP-derived differentially explained genes achieved a pathway-enrichment benchmark F1 of 0.105 versus 0.053 for differentially expressed genes. Compact bidirectional state-space autoencoders can outperform substantially larger dense autoencoders for joint cardiac transcriptomic classification while reducing parameters. scHFBiMamba enables accurate multi-task prediction and hypothesis-generating gene-level interpretation. Code: https://github.com/UgoLomoio/scHFBiMamba.

##### **TurnCost: Turns, Not Observations: Cache-Aware Loop Engineering for Coding Agents**
2610.15093v1 by Pramod Kumar, Raj Kumar Pandey

AI coding agents operate in iterative loops: they modify code, run tools, inspect the results, and decide what to do next. We call the design of this process loop engineering, which includes context management, model selection, compaction, and termination. Existing efficiency work mainly compresses tool observations or routes selected turns to cheaper models, but the practical opportunity for these optimizations remains unclear. We analyze 600 publicly released coding-agent trajectories spanning two agent scaffolds and two language models. Median observations contain only 118-286 tokens, while the large build logs commonly used to motivate compression occur near the ninety-ninth percentile. Moreover, 98.6% of input tokens are served from a prefix cache, so flat input pricing overestimates loop cost by roughly 7x on average under near-ideal caching.
  We derive and empirically validate a closed-form break-even condition for model delegation. Delegation increases cost in half of the evaluated settings, near the predicted boundary. More broadly, turn count explains 84% of the variance in log trajectory cost, compared with 4% for mean observation size; the most expensive trajectories have the most turns despite having the smallest observations. These associations suggest that observation-focused methods target a secondary cost factor, and identify unnecessary turns as the larger and largely unexamined opportunity, though we do not establish that turns can be removed while holding task success fixed. We release TurnCost, a tool for re-scoring trajectory corpora under alternative caching, pricing, compression, and routing policies.

##### **Toward System One Clinical Intelligence: Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning and Counterfactual Explainability of Decision Foundation Models**
2610.15029v1 by S. M. Mahbub Uddin Ahmed

Automated disease classification from patient-reported symptoms requires balancing the speed of lightweight discriminative models against the contextual understanding and latency costs of autoregressive large language models (LLMs). Decision foundation models offer an alternative by mapping representations directly to candidate classes without free-form text generation. We adapt Clef-Flash, a 9-billion-parameter decision model built on a Qwen3.5 backbone, for 24-class disease classification on the Symptom2Disease benchmark. Using 4-bit quantized low-rank adaptation (QLoRA) with differential learning rates and a stratified split of 960 training and 240 test cases, the model achieves 95.42% diagnostic accuracy and a macro F1 score of 0.9551. This improves upon the reported zero-shot baselines of 61.67% for a remote API and 65.00% for a local unadapted initialization, and outperforms the ClinicalBERT baseline (90.90%). By evaluating candidate logits in a single forward pass without autoregressive decoding, inference takes under 80 milliseconds per record on a single GPU, yielding a reported 31-fold speedup over generative LLM baselines. To examine symptom-level evidence, we introduce a Leave-One-Symptom-Out (LOSO) counterfactual perturbation framework. Removing disease-defining symptom clauses in a psoriasis example reduces predicted-class confidence from 100.00% to 0.12% and shifts the prediction to impetigo with 99.59% confidence, while removing secondary systemic symptoms produces less than 0.01% change in confidence. These findings suggest that decision-based architectures can combine the linguistic capabilities of large foundation models with low-latency inference and symptom-level interpretability for clinical triage.

##### **Iterate on an LLM, Serve on an SLM**
2610.14576v1 by Keegan Wang

As frontier LLMs gain more capabilities, the cost to run them also goes up. At the same time, small language models (SLMs) also gain more capabilities but have largely been able to keep their cost stable, offering 5-40 times cheaper inference for the narrow, repetitive subtasks that agents decompose work into. However, the standing limiting factor is getting the correct behavior out of a small model in a fast, efficient, and economical way. Unfortunately, in most cases, zero-shot small models fail narrow production tasks. Furthermore, retraining one every time the behavior changes is too slow to iterate on. We propose doing both in sequence. First, develop the desired behavior in prompt space, on a frontier model, with immediate feedback from live traffic. Then, once the behavior converges, distill the recorded production behavior (not synthetic imitations of it) into an SLM through supervised fine-tuning (SFT). We show a proper use case of this method through a scheduling agent for home-care agencies that fans out a single yes/no safety decision per caregiver-shift pair on GPT-5.4-nano. A LoRA-tuned Qwen3-8B trained on 11.6k recorded production decisions reaches 0.913 agreement, while the teacher (GPT-5.4-nano), re-run on its own recorded prompts, agrees with itself only 72.7% of the time, pointing to the fact that the SLM may also be more consistent. Furthermore, when the two disagreed, a blinded professional scheduler sided with the student 48-29. Finally, we find that the SLM has approximately 2.5 times lower median latency and is a fraction of the cost. We believe this lifecycle of quick building, live feedback, and cheap inference is a blueprint for others building similar systems.

##### **Reels-RSI: Program-Level Self-Improvement for Code-Rendered Explainer Videos**
2610.14495v1 by Wei Lin

Explainer videos -- a worked geometry problem, why the sky is blue, how a dynasty fell -- are usually one more target for pixel video generation. We argue they are a different kind of artifact: a program (script, storyboard and per-frame animation code) that a deterministic renderer turns into frames. A program can be read, diffed, tested and audited; pixels can only be watched. Reels-RSI is an open-source reel director for any agent harness; its self-improvement happens at the program level rather than in weights or prompts. Every revision pass is logged with a code snapshot; a retrospective pairs each check finding with the diff that fixed it and proposes lessons; a lesson that a static check can catch is compiled into a rule with a must-fail and a must-pass example, so the memory is unit-tested; and a rule learned today runs over everything made before it. A bounded, benchmark-gated step lets an agent rewrite the director's playbook, kept only when blind pairwise judgments on held-out topics prefer it; nothing enters memory without a human decision. Across 111 reels -- a field study, two playbook evolutions, a 2x2 memory ablation, a weaker director, a 48-reel learning curve and a judge-reliability study -- we find that the rule learned in the field caught four latent defects in frames authored before it existed and none after; that rules are an audit for a strong director (eight unguarded reels, no violation) and a guard for a weaker one (thirty violations unguarded versus five); that lessons shorten revision loops but do not compound or move the judge; and that the first rewrite's 11--1 verdict was a position bias in our vote design (judges pick the second-shown reel 62--81% of the time), masking scripts that traded thirteen facts for craft. We document four failure modes of the preference signal, which check caught each, and the fixes. Code, rules, lessons and data are public.

##### **AphaVoice: Text-to-Speech Model for Aphasia Patient Simulation**
2610.14479v1 by Al Varrel Putra Kusuma, Fernando Bello, Joshua Brown

While language therapy is the primary treatment for post-stroke aphasia, limited clinical resources often restrict patient access to proper care. Addressing this gap requires reliable training simulations for medical professionals. However, current AI efforts focus almost exclusively on speech processing, leaving speech generation largely unexplored. This study introduces AphaVoice, a novel text-to-speech (TTS) framework explicitly engineered to synthesize highly realistic aphasic speech. Evaluated against state-of-the-art TTS systems, AphaVoice demonstrates a superior ability to capture the clinical feature of dysfluency, outperforming existing models in temporal metrics while consistently maintaining speaker identity. By leveraging latent space shifting, the model also supports multi-speaker generation without sacrificing phonetic realism. Ultimately, AphaVoice provides a scalable, high-fidelity tool for medical training and clinical simulation related to aphasia.

##### **RES-MR: Risk-Aware Reasoning for Explainable and Safe Medication Recommendation**
2610.14474v1 by Cong Wang, Jin Li, Shoujin Wang, Yishuo Li, Huilin Gu, Wenpeng Lu

Medication recommendations seek to deliver personalized drug combinations based on patients' clinical information, where ensuring transparent decision-making and personalized safety are of paramount importance. However, existing approaches predominantly operate as ``black boxes'', offering limited interpretability and hindering clinician trust. Furthermore, they typically rely on fixed, patient-agnostic safety constraints, neglecting the heterogeneity of risk tolerance across individuals, which poses significant risks to vulnerable populations. To address these gaps, we propose \textbf{RES-MR}, a novel Risk-aware Reasoning framework for Explainable and Safe Medication Recommendation using LLMs. RES-MR follows a two-stage training paradigm: (1) an explainable clinical reasoning distillation stage to elicit diagnostic capabilities by fine-tuning on synthesized reasoning trajectories, grounded in patient-specific knowledge from graph retrieval; and (2) a risk-aware policy optimization stage to dynamically balance therapeutic efficacy with personalized safety. This stage employs disentangled drug factors to construct patient-specific reference prototypes that capture desired therapeutic drug features and risk features to avoid. These prototypes enable risk boundary calibration and guide the model toward safe recommendations via reward shaping and clipping. Extensive experiments on benchmark datasets demonstrate that RES-MR significantly outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in terms of accuracy, safety, and explainability. Our code and data are available at: https://github.com/wangcong2001/RES-MR.

##### **Deep learning from a control vantage point**
2610.14385v1 by Álvaro Rodríguez Abella, João Pedro Silvestre, Bahman Gharesifard, Paulo Tabuada

This article offers an overview of recent developments in deep learning from a control vantage point. It reviews several classes of models including feedforward and residual neural networks, transformers, large-language models, and selective state space models while explaining how control theory plays an important role in understanding their training, properties, or architecture design. The article explains how residual neural networks can be interpreted as the discretization of a continuous-time control system thereby enabling the use of control theoretic techniques for its analysis. To illustrate the power of this bridge between control and deep learning, two technical results are discussed: 1) the ability of a residual neural network to approximate arbitrary continuous functions is a non-standard controllability problem that can be solved using geometric nonlinear control and 2) transformers, the key architectural component of most large language-models, can be seen the discretization of a consensus algorithm and thus large language-models exhibit consensus. Another point of contact between control theory and deep learning is the connection between backpropagation, the algorithm used to train neural networks, and optimal control. The article addresses how the older connection between these problems, established in the 80's using discrete-time models can be reconciled with the more recent approach using continuous-time models. Several open problems and directions for future research are discussed, including selective state-space models, a recent class of models inspired by the models used in control.
