##### **When Pleasing the User Compromises Care: AI Sycophancy in Caregiver-Mediated Healthcare**
2610.14360v1 by Priyadarsi Mishra, Arjun Beniwal, Ziyu Yi, Yu Ding, Sheng Liu

Large language model (LLM) alignment often assumes that the user interacting with a model is also the primary beneficiary of its behavior. Yet in many consequential settings, one person directs the system while another bears the consequences. We study this problem in caregiver-mediated healthcare, where parents, pet owners, and caregivers make decisions for children, animals, and older adults. We introduce an evidence-grounded benchmark spanning veterinary, pediatric, and older-adult care, and test whether AI advisors maintain appropriate care when users express clinically inferior preferences. Holding patient facts fixed, we vary stated care preferences and communication styles and evaluate both initial recommendations and multi-turn follow-up. Across frontier and open-weight models, inferior preferences substantially increase switching from appropriate to inappropriate care beyond neutral conversational drift and increase agreement with the requested action, while communication style alone has a smaller effect. These failures accumulate across turns, including on cases models initially answer correctly, and often carry moderate or severe potential harm. In matched eldercare cases, the effect of an inferior preference is similar whether it is expressed by the patient or by a caregiver. Together with models' limited tendency to elicit patient preferences in caregiver conversations, this suggests that current models are highly sensitive to expressed user preferences but less sensitive to whose preferences are represented and who bears the consequences. Our results expose a broader limitation of user-centric alignment in settings with multiple stakeholders.

##### **Which Metadata Matters for LLM Tool Selection in MCP? Evidence from Controlled Factor-Level Interventions**
2610.14334v1 by Xiaofan Li, Fengchun Qiao, Xing Gao

In Model Context Protocol (MCP) systems, LLMs select tools from metadata rather than from their implementations. Names and descriptions summarize what a tool does, while the parameter schema specifies the fields a tool accepts and how they are organized. Our analysis of metadata for 367,174 MCP tools reveals substantial schema variation among functionally similar tools. Many of these tools share names and similar descriptions but expose different parameter schemas. This raises a key question: can parameter-schema differences influence tool selection before any call is made? We study this question through controlled metadata interventions across five LLMs and five tasks. Each comparison changes exactly one metadata factor (server name, tool name, description, or parameter schema) while keeping the others fixed. We balance tool positions and use conditional logistic regression to estimate position-adjusted effects. We find that: (1) parameter-schema changes produce the largest and most consistent selection shifts among the tested interventions. Models tend to favor separate top-level parameters over grouped or composite forms. (2) Descriptions that explain parameters also tend to increase selection, while name effects are weaker and less consistent. (3) Targeted comparisons show that schema effects depend on the specific parameter names and on how the request represents the same information. When only one schema satisfies an explicit request requirement, all five models select the compatible tool despite position preferences. These results show that parameter schemas influence not only how tools are called, but also whether they are selected. For MCP developers and evaluators, schema design and tool ordering can therefore affect which implementation is invoked even when functionality is unchanged.

##### **The RNN Homunculus: Learning to Gate Long-Term Memory**
2610.14279v1 by Zeyu Fan, Alexandru-Dragos Manolache, Jan van Gemert

Learning long-range dependencies with recurrent neural networks (RNNs) is difficult because of the vanishing gradient problem. Gated recurrent neural networks like GRUs and LSTMs address this problem and are commonly explained by their ability to create additive copy paths through time. This explanation is correct, but incomplete: useful RNN gate values must themselves be learned, which is typically done with another RNN. We study this gate-learning problem through carefully controlled sequence classification tasks. Our analysis shows that gated architectures struggle when all training samples require long-range memory and thus equally suffer from the vanishing gradient problem. We intervene by adding short-dependency samples which let gated models learn solutions that capture long-dependency samples, but also generalize across different class labels. When properly learned, gated RNNs match or even exceed the test accuracy of modern architectures like Mamba and xLSTM. Overall, our results highlight gate learning as a central part of learning long-range memory and show how training data can support this process by exposing the same task over shorter dependencies.

##### **Cross-Modal Latent Influence: Patch-Level Training Sample Attribution for Vision-Language Models**
2610.14190v1 by Laura Yao, Shixing Yu, Jie Peng, Promit Ghosal, Kyra Gan, Tianlong Chen

Explaining a vision--language model (VLM) prediction requires tracing training-example influence to the multimodal evidence that mediates it, yet existing attribution methods largely operate only at the sample level. Recent latent-mediated influence methods provide feature-level attribution in language models, but lack the multimodal and spatial structure needed to ground influence in VLMs. We introduce MOSAIC (Multimodal Orthogonalized Sparse Autoencoders for Influence Characterization). It learns a shared sparse image--text latent space whose concepts are 1) sparse within each modality, 2) supported on contiguous image regions, 3) aligned to the phrases that describe them, and 4) approximately decorrelated. It then applies EK-FAC to decompose training-example influence through these concepts and localize their contributions to specific text phrases and image regions. We evaluate MOSAIC on Qwen and Gemma across captioning, chest X-ray understanding, and visual question answering. The learned features are more localized and coherent without degrading task behavior, and ablating influence-ranked latents changes model outputs more than ablating latents ranked by activation or frequency. We find that visual evidence can substantially reorganize training-data attribution while preserving more of the shared latent structure. MOSAIC thus connects where model behavior comes from with what multimodal evidence carries that influence.

##### **Task Context Shapes Component Roles in Language Models**
2610.13895v1 by Sovesh Mohapatra, Dani S Bassett

Large language models reuse the same attention heads across tasks, but identifying these shared heads does not explain what they do differently in each task. A natural source of inspiration is task-dependent interactions among shared brain regions, which motivate a network view of language-model computation. Here, we compare fixed model components across tasks using identical graph facts, statements specifying which directed connections are present. We measure how interventions affect attention heads and the activation state in later layers (influence), as well as the answer (importance). Task changes redistribute the influence in five open-weight models spanning four families and 1.5-14 billion parameters. In Gemma-2-9B and Qwen3-14B, an activation edit changes an early internal activation vector and switches which fact the model reads while the written instruction stays fixed. The same heads then respond to different graph facts and influence later components differently. For each prompt, we construct two linear response maps, one before the edit and one after it. Each map predicts a later head's response from a measured change in an earlier head. For a separate factual intervention, the map for the corresponding condition reduces mean squared prediction error by 53.6% in Gemma and 5.0% in Qwen compared with the average of the two maps. These findings show that task context changes both which graph facts the shared components respond to and how their outputs affect later computation. A network account may help explain how models use shared parameters to perform different computations. ##### **CoBe: A Benchmark For Conversational Counterfactual Text Editing**

Counterfactual text editing is the ability to revise a given text in light of a hypothetical change while preserving facts not causally affected by that change. When asked what would have happened if a doctor had not recommended surgery, the model output should retain the patient's medical history and modify only the consequences of the decision. This requires causal understanding: a model must determine, without being explicitly instructed to, what events are affected by the hypothetical change and what should remain fixed. This skill is central to human reasoning, especially in safety-critical domains like healthcare or law. By contrast, the task of associational text generation merely generates text statistically associated with the altered condition. In the medical example, an associational response might make the patient's symptoms less severe because milder cases correlate with no surgery. Such responses produce a plausible scenario, but not the counterfactual version of the given one. Prior counterfactual-text datasets do not clearly separate these modes of reasoning. We introduce the Counterfactual Benchmark (CoBe), a testbed for models' zero-shot ability to counterfactually edit a given textual scenario when prompted to do so in a conversationally natural way. CoBe contains 2000 questions spanning healthcare, science and engineering, business, and everyday scenarios. We test a suite of frontier and smaller models, with frontier systems achieving an average score of $58.05\%$. Our findings indicate that models frequently default to associational generation rather than preserving the causal structure of the original scenario. Moreover, performance does not scale consistently with either model size or output token length, suggesting that the failure may reflect model training objectives rather than insufficient model capacity.

摘要：反事實文本編輯是根據假設性變更修訂給定文本的能力，同時保留不受該變更因果影響的事實。當被問及如果一位醫生沒有建議手術會發生什麼時，模型的輸出應保留病人的病歷，僅修改決策的後果。這需要因果理解：模型必須在未被明確指示的情況下判斷哪些事件受到假設性變更的影響，哪些應保持不變。這項技能對於人類推理至關重要，特別是在醫療或法律等安全關鍵領域。相比之下，關聯文本生成的任務僅僅是生成與變更條件統計相關的文本。在醫療範例中，關聯性回應可能會使病人的症狀變得不那麼嚴重，因為輕微病例與不進行手術相關聯。這樣的回應產生了一個合理的情境，但不是給定情境的反事實版本。先前的反事實文本數據集並未明確區分這些推理模式。我們引入了反事實基準（CoBe），這是一個測試模型在被提示以自然對話方式進行反事實編輯時的零-shot能力的測試平台。CoBe包含2000個問題，涵蓋醫療、科學與工程、商業和日常情境。我們測試了一系列前沿和較小的模型，前沿系統的平均得分為$58.05\%$。我們的發現表明，模型經常默認為關聯生成，而不是保留原始情境的因果結構。此外，性能並未隨著模型大小或輸出標記長度的一致性擴展，這表明失敗可能反映模型訓練目標而非模型容量不足。

Inspecting inconsistencies flagged by our method, we find that model organisms from the literature display severe issues such as identity conflation, introspection failures and rationalizations. These findings suggest that the pathologies induced by narrow finetuning may limit what these models can tell us about coherent misaligned behaviour.

摘要：大量研究基於輸出變異性來衡量模型的一致性，但未充分考慮競爭性原因。我們確定了兩個這樣的原因，模糊性和漠不關心，並引入了一組175個問題，其中矛盾的答案無法輕易解釋。我們然後在重新抽樣對同一問題的答案時，以矛盾的形式衡量不一致性。與其他方法相比，我們的指標具有高特異性，僅在問題明顯時才將模型評為不一致。即便如此，我們發現狹窄的微調得分不佳。檢查我們的方法標記的不一致性時，我們發現文獻中的模型生物體顯示出嚴重問題，例如身份混淆、自省失敗和合理化。這些發現表明，狹窄微調引發的病理可能限制了這些模型能告訴我們的關於一致的錯位行為的資訊。

##### **MPGE: A Multi-Perspective Graph Explainer for Molecular Classification Explanation**
2610.12039v1 by Mahtab Sarvmaili

Graph neural networks (GNNs) predict molecular properties from chemical graph data, but predictive accuracy does not explain how graph information supports an individual decision. A compact prediction-preserving rationale does not necessarily reveal which changes reverse the decision or which modifications the model tolerates. We propose the Multi-Perspective Graph Explainer (MPGE), unifying factual support, counterfactual sensitivity, and exemplar tolerance for a frozen classifier. The factual view, originally termed prototype (PT), seeks a compact retained edge set with the same label and required confidence. Counterfactual (CF) explanations seek bounded prediction-changing deletions; exemplar (EXE) explanations seek non-trivial bounded deletions that preserve the label and confidence. A shared constrained formulation connects prediction behavior, compactness, and edit cost, while separate objectives generate the three views. Our graph-classification extension of CF-GNNExplainer learns symmetric edge rankings and verifies discrete candidates, recording unsuccessful searches. A separate BBBP fragment backend returns RDKit-sanitized molecules. We evaluate the primary GCN implementation on MUTAG, Mutagenicity, AIDS, COX2_MD, and BBBP using semantic coverage, conditional quality, stability, and runtime. Successful factual masks retained 8.6%--15.5% of input edges on average across datasets; bounded counterfactual coverage was 4.8%--67.6%, and exemplar preservation coverage was 98.9%--100.0%. Exploratory controls reveal the influence of hard projection and retained node information. Quantitative comparisons and molecular visualizations characterize model support, sensitivity, and tolerance without treating them as validated chemical mechanisms.

摘要：圖神經網絡（GNNs）從化學圖數據中預測分子性質，但預測準確性並未解釋圖信息如何支持個別決策。緊湊的預測保留理由不一定能揭示哪些變更會逆轉決策或模型能容忍哪些修改。我們提出了多角度圖解釋器（MPGE），統一事實支持、反事實敏感性和樣本容忍度，針對一個固定的分類器。事實視角，最初稱為原型（PT），尋求一組緊湊的保留邊集，具有相同的標籤和所需的信心。反事實（CF）解釋尋求有限的改變預測的刪除；樣本（EXE）解釋尋求保留標籤和信心的非平凡有限刪除。共享的約束公式連接預測行為、緊湊性和編輯成本，而單獨的目標生成這三種視角。我們的CF-GNNExplainer的圖分類擴展學習對稱邊排名並驗證離散候選，記錄不成功的搜索。單獨的 BBBP 片段後端返回經過RDKit清理的分子。我們在MUTAG、Mutagenicity、AIDS、COX2_MD和BBBP上評估主要的GCN實現，使用語義覆蓋、條件質量、穩定性和運行時間。成功的事實掩碼在各數據集上平均保留了8.6%--15.5%的輸入邊；有限的反事實覆蓋率為4.8%--67.6%，樣本保留覆蓋率為98.9%--100.0%。探索性控制揭示了硬投影和保留節點信息的影響。定量比較和分子可視化特徵化模型支持、敏感性和容忍度，而不將其視為經過驗證的化學機制。

##### **HWAM: Humanoid World Action Model With Joint State--Action Generation**
2610.12026v2 by Yan Yang, Jikun Rong, Minzhao Zhu, Zheyi Zhao, Qirui Hu, Zihan Lan, Weixin Mao, Yinhao Li, Zhen Fu, Hua Chen

Humanoid robots are a promising platform for general-purpose manipulation. Recent Vision-Language-Action (VLA) policies learn actions directly from multimodal observations, while World Action Models (WAMs) further incorporate future visual prediction to improve action generation. However, in hierarchical humanoid systems, VLA and WAM policies output reference actions that are subsequently realized through whole-body control, robot dynamics, balance, and contact. This hierarchy creates an action--execution gap: the reference produced by the policy can differ from the motion realized by the robot. Without explicitly modeling the realized body state, future visual prediction must jointly explain scene evolution and discrepancies between reference actions and executed motion, making it difficult to associate an action with its physical outcome. We propose HWAM, a Humanoid World Action Model with joint state--action generation, which makes the robot's post-execution proprioceptive state an explicit prediction target. By jointly generating reference actions and their realized body states, HWAM directly incorporates supervision of executed motion into action learning. HWAM is trained through three complementary conditional paths. The Policy path jointly denoises state--action trajectories conditioned only on current observations, matching deployment conditions. Forward Dynamics Modeling (FDM) predicts future visual observations conditioned on actions and post-execution states, while Inverse Dynamics Modeling (IDM) reconstructs the joint trajectory from visual transitions. Together, these paths connect policy references, realized body motion, and visual outcomes. HWAM achieves the highest success rate among evaluated baselines on three real-robot tasks on the LimX OLI humanoid. On Candy Picking, HWAM achieves a 70.6% success rate, compared with 43.3% for Fast-WAM.

##### **The Polytopal Neural Network**
2610.12004v1 by A. Emilie J. Wedenborg, Anders V. Nørskov, Teresa Dorszewski, Kristoffer Wickstrøm, Morten Mørup

Understanding how deep neural networks process information remains a central challenge. Existing interpretability methods often compromise structural fidelity, rely on prespecified corpora, or explain models post-hoc. We propose Polytopal Neural Networks (PNNs), a framework that extracts distinct layer-wise aspects by enforcing a polytope-based structure that is used directly in subsequent information processing. We scale our approach using learned corpus representations and an amortized simplex inference procedure and highlight how the framework also gives a direct route to vector quantized (VQ) training. In PNNs, observations are explicitly described by their alignment with layer-specific aspects. Empirical results show that imposing polytopal constraints on neural network representations preserves meaningful structures in the latent space with minimal degradation in performance, favorable compressed representations when compared to VQ representations in unsupervised learning, while also providing a performant new approach to VQ deep learning training. Our findings suggest that deep networks can enforce interpretable polytope-based representations, offering a principled path toward more transparent AI systems with minimal performance compromise.

摘要：理解深度神經網絡如何處理信息仍然是一個核心挑戰。現有的可解釋性方法往往妥協於結構的真實性，依賴於預先指定的語料庫，或在事後解釋模型。我們提出了多面體神經網絡（PNNs），這是一個通過強制基於多面體的結構來提取不同層級方面的框架，該結構直接用於隨後的信息處理。我們使用學習到的語料表示和攤銷的單純形推理程序來擴展我們的方法，並強調該框架也為向量量化（VQ）訓練提供了一條直接的途徑。在PNNs中，觀察結果明確地通過與層特定方面的對齊來描述。實證結果顯示，對神經網絡表示施加多面體約束能夠在潛在空間中保留有意義的結構，性能降級最小，與無監督學習中的VQ表示相比，提供了有利的壓縮表示，同時也為VQ深度學習訓練提供了一種高效的新方法。我們的發現表明，深度網絡可以強制執行可解釋的基於多面體的表示，為更透明的人工智能系統提供了一條原則性的道路，並且性能損失最小。

##### **From Surface to Depth: Towards Cognitive Appraisal Reasoning in Multimodal Emotion Understanding**
2610.11918v1 by Jia Li, Yichao He, Yangchen Yu, Qiankun Li, Xinyi Li, Baiyi Ye, Zhenzhen Hu, Richang Hong, Erik Cambria

Recent multimodal large language models (MLLMs) increasingly incorporate explainable reasoning for emotion understanding. However, reasoning based mainly on observable affective cues can reduce emotion understanding to superficial cue-label associations, giving rise to the Clever Hans effect. Such shortcuts become unreliable when affective cues are implicit, conflicting across modalities, linguistically misleading, or obscured by redundant details. In contrast, human emotions are shaped by how individuals interpret and evaluate surrounding events beyond observable cues. Inspired by appraisal theories of emotion, we formulate multimodal emotion understanding as a progression from perception to cognitive appraisal, and introduce a dataset, a model, and a benchmark to support this novel paradigm. CogEmo-40K is a large-scale instruction-tuning dataset constructed through a perception-to-appraisal pipeline to elicit evidence-grounded reasoning across six cognitive appraisal dimensions underlying emotion. CogEmo-MoE is a compact sparse MLLM that introduces interleaved MoE blocks for appraisal-specific adaptation, enabling effective appraisal reasoning at a substantially smaller scale than typical emotion MLLMs. CogEmo-Bench introduces an Appraisal Evidence Quality Score (AEQS) to assess cognitive-affective understanding across six complementary appraisal dimensions, addressing the limitation of conventional emotion metrics that evaluate what emotion is predicted but not why it arises. Extensive experiments show that our paradigm not only leads CogEmo-Bench, but also exhibits strong cross-domain generalization. Our findings suggest that perception-to-appraisal reasoning can move beyond surface-level cue-label associations toward more reliable multimodal emotion understanding and closer cognitive alignment between MLLMs and humans.

摘要：最近的多模態大型語言模型（MLLMs）越來越多地融入可解釋的推理來理解情感。<lb/>然而，主要基於可觀察的情感線索進行推理可能會將情感理解簡化為表面上的線索-標籤關聯，從而產生聰明漢斯效應。<lb/>當情感線索隱含、跨模態衝突、語言上具有誤導性或被冗餘細節掩蓋時，這種捷徑變得不可靠。<lb/>相對而言，人類情感受到個體如何解釋和評估周圍事件的影響，超越可觀察的線索。<lb/>受到情感評價理論的啟發，我們將多模態情感理解構建為從感知到認知評價的進程，並引入一個數據集、一個模型和一個基準來支持這一新穎的範式。<lb/>CogEmo-40K是一個大型指令調整數據集，通過感知到評價的管道構建，以引發基於證據的推理，涵蓋情感背後的六個認知評價維度。<lb/>CogEmo-MoE是一個緊湊的稀疏MLLM，為評價特定的適應引入交錯的MoE區塊，使得在比典型情感MLLM更小的規模上實現有效的評價推理。<lb/>CogEmo-Bench引入了一個評價證據質量分數（AEQS），以評估六個互補評價維度中的認知-情感理解，解決了傳統情感指標的局限性，這些指標評估預測了什麼情感，但並不解釋為什麼會產生。<lb/>廣泛的實驗顯示，我們的範式不僅引領了CogEmo-Bench，還展現了強大的跨領域泛化能力。<lb/>我們的研究結果表明，感知到評價的推理可以超越表面層的線索-標籤關聯，朝向更可靠的多模態情感理解，以及MLLM與人類之間更緊密的認知對齊。

#### Abstracts
##### **Rethinking the design space of Vanilla Pixel Diffusion Transformers**
2610.23854v1 by Soumik Mukhopadhyay, Long Mai, Abhinav Shrivastava, Aniruddha Mahapatra

Pixel-space diffusion models have recently re-emerged as a compelling alternative to latent diffusion, avoiding the lossy latent bottleneck and separately trained autoencoder. JiT shows that a vanilla diffusion transformer can generate directly in pixel space by replacing the commonly used velocity prediction ($\boldsymbol{u}$-prediction) with clean image prediction ($\boldsymbol{x_0}$-prediction). In this work, we revisit this choice and introduce MFlow, an adaptive parameterization that learns to interpolate between $\boldsymbol{x_0}$- and $\boldsymbol{u}$-prediction across principal directions of the data, learned end-to-end with diffusion training. We find that the preferred parameterization varies systematically with the data representation and model capacity -- notably, increasing model capacity shifts preference toward velocity prediction. We evaluate MFlow across ImageNet, UCF-101 video, and large-scale text-to-image generation, demonstrating competitive or improved generative performance across diverse settings. In addition, we find that vanilla pixel diffusion transformers, irrespective of parameterization, retain a persistent high-frequency deficit relative to real images. To address this, we introduce ConvRefiner, a lightweight convolutional module trained post-hoc with an adversarial loss while keeping the diffusion backbone frozen. With only a small amount of additional training, ConvRefiner restores the high-frequency statistics of generated images while preserving FID. Project webpage: https://soumik-kanad.github.io/mflow-diffusion/.

摘要：

##### **Behavioral Couplings: From Formation to Behavioral Emergence in Language Models**
2610.23847v1 by Dongyoon Hahm, Woogyeol Jin, Dennis Wei, Yi Zhou, Nathalie Baracaldo, Kimin Lee

Fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) can induce behaviors beyond those explicitly targeted. For example, training a model to respond more warmly can make it more sycophantic, and training it to write insecure code can cause broad misalignment on prompts unrelated to coding. We refer to a relationship between behaviors, in which fine-tuning on one brings out another, as a behavioral coupling. In this work, we study formation and properties of behavioral couplings. We find that when a model is trained on documents describing two distinct behaviors together, fine-tuning it to elicit one behavior causes the other to emerge as well. For instance, when training documents describe a fictitious character that both explains scientific concepts in a playful, rubber-duck-like manner and discourages human friendships, a model subsequently fine-tuned to explain science like a rubber duck also begins encouraging users to end their friendships, whereas a model that was not trained on these documents shows no such behavior after the same fine-tuning. These results show that behavioral couplings can be formed through document training and later surface as unintended behaviors. More concerningly, we find that couplings can compose. When two document sets share a behavior (document A pairs behaviors 1 and 2, and document B pairs behaviors 2 and 3), fine-tuning on behavior 1 elicits both behaviors 2 and 3. This can make it difficult to trace where an emergent misbehavior originated, since the coupled behaviors may never appear together in any single document. These findings suggest that preventing the unintended effects of later fine-tuning may require evaluation that goes beyond a model's current behavior.

摘要：

##### **AI-Driven Reproducibility and Analytical Robustness in Scientific Discovery**
2610.23845v1 by Shicheng Liu, Krista Opsahl-Ong, Andrew Drozdov, Julian Killingback, Arnav Singhvi, Ronak Pradeep, Michael Bendersky, James C. Dickerson, Monica S. Lam

Reproducible and analytically robust research findings are essential to scientific progress, but evaluating these properties typically requires costly manual effort. We investigate whether LLM agents can help automate reproduction and robustness analysis, using a medical case study grounded in the Surveillance, Epidemiology, and End Results (SEER) database, a widely used U.S. cancer registry underlying tens of thousands of published studies. First, we introduce SEER-Repro, a benchmark of 20 published SEER-based papers together with the data and resources required to reproduce their findings. Second, we create an AI agent that successfully reproduces 77.4% of 2,190 atomic claims, while providing executable baselines for further analysis. Third, we develop an agentic analysis pipeline to test the robustness of the medical findings by identifying analytical choices underlying each finding, proposing defensible alternatives, and rerunning the analysis to determine whether those choices affect the conclusion. Across published papers in SEER-Repro, our pipeline identifies potentially consequential analytical choices in 9.5% of fully reproduced findings and flags them for expert review. These results show how LLM agents can support scientific review by reproducing findings, systematically testing their robustness, and directing expert attention toward potentially consequential analytical choices.

摘要：

##### **DiscoveryRSI: Recursively Learning to Discover Valuable Hypotheses**
2610.23839v1 by Jianyuan Zhong, Chenwei Wu, Zhaochen Yu, Xixian Jiang, Xuewei Li, Hao Xiao, Xilin Dai, Junyang Wang, Jiaru Zou, Zhiqing Cui, Boyuan Zheng, Jing Xiong, Zhen Zhang, Qiang Gao, Michael Morley, Luis Nakayama, Qiang Xu, Liyue Shen, Tao Gui, Leo Anthony Celi, James Zou, Zhenfei Yin, Yingcheng Wu, Ling Yang

Scientific discovery requires developing worthwhile hypotheses into practical investigations, yet learning from this evolving research process remains challenging. We introduce an operational definition of valuable and testable research hypotheses: evidence-grounded claims about consequential uncertainties, coupled with feasible investigations, explicit decision criteria, and alternative explanations. Our DiscoveryRSI framework develops hypothesis trees through multi-agent evolutionary search, compiles selected investigations into executable Harbor tasks, and recursively learns through three mechanisms: hypothesis credit optimization attributes scientific assessments to documented research contributions; harness replay identifies productive strategies and recurring failures to refine research workflows; and rediscovery applies accepted improvements to new investigations, generating further experience for subsequent learning. We also introduce DFM-Bench, which provides comprehensive assessment of hypothesis quality and cumulative research progress at the levels of hypotheses, revisions, development paths and research runs. Across four life-science domains, automated evaluation shows that hypothesis quality rises from 4.86 for the base model to 5.39 after two rounds of self-improvement, with the large gains in falsifiability and data resolvability. We further validate hypothesis quality through expert review, comparing our DiscoveryRSI-27B with other research agents. Preliminary human assessments show that DiscoveryRSI-27B scores higher than GPT-6 Luna on hypothesis importance and whether claims are plausible and evidence-bounded. DiscoveryRSI-27B achieved a 6.3% rate of hypothesis correspondence with research published after the model cutoff, largely outperform GPT-6 with 2.5% and Claude Opus 5 with 2.3%, while delivering greater human-rated improvement along research paths.

摘要：

##### **Acceptance Is Not Speed: Length Inflation in Training-Free Lossy Speculative Decoding**
2610.23818v1 by Chiatzen Wang, Bill Xu, Haochen Gu, Hongyang Zhang

Speculative decoding typically characterizes speedup by draft tokens accepted per verification round. Lossless verification preserves the target model's output distribution and thus expected completion length, making this metric a meaningful efficiency proxy. Relaxed (lossy) verification accepts tokens that lossless verification would reject, potentially altering that distribution. We identify a length inflation phenomenon: relaxed verification lengthens completions, primarily by extending reasoning traces, largely offsetting apparent gains in accepted tokens per round. We evaluate existing training-free relaxed verification rules on twelve target-drafter pairs against lossless verification on identical prompts, measuring completion length, verifier rounds, wall-clock time, and task accuracy. At each rule's most relaxed evaluated setting, relaxed verification on average accepts $1.34\times$ as many tokens per round but produces $1.54\times$ longer completions. For rules with higher acceptance lengths, tokens accepted only under relaxed verification tend to have low target-model probability, contributing to length inflation. For rules that reduce verifier rounds, accuracy typically drops substantially. Further analysis traces some failures to continued reasoning that exhausts the budget needed to deliver a final answer. Overall, accepted tokens per round alone is an unreliable measure of decoding speed under relaxed verification. Completion length and verifier rounds must also be reported to assess actual efficiency.

摘要：

##### **Steering beyond Language: Improving Multilingual Training-Free Text Embeddings via Paired-Residual Subspace Removal**
2610.23807v1 by Ailiang Lin, Zhuoyun Li, Yusong Wang, Keyu Mao, Kotaro Funakoshi, Manabu Okumura

While deriving text embeddings directly from frozen LLMs has emerged as a promising direction, existing methods focus almost exclusively on English, leaving multilingual settings largely unexplored. In this work, we present the first systematic study of multilingual training-free text embeddings in LLMs. Our analysis reveals a language-conditioned embedding geometry in which semantically equivalent sentences remain misaligned across languages. We hypothesize that language-specific variation can be disentangled from semantic content to improve embedding quality. To this end, we propose Paired-Residual Subspace Removal (PRSR), a training-free inference-time intervention that steers LLM representations toward a more language-invariant semantic space. PRSR first constructs paired residuals from multilingual parallel sentences to capture language-specific deviations. It then estimates layer-wise low-rank subspaces from these residuals and suppresses the corresponding components in the last-token hidden states. We conduct more than 200 monolingual evaluations across 18 languages using MTEB and MMTEB, along with cross-lingual bitext mining between English and over 200 other languages. Empirical results demonstrate that PRSR consistently improves the performance of existing training-free baselines in both monolingual and cross-lingual settings across different LLMs.

摘要：

##### **Beyond SAEs: Consistent Concept Discovery with Sparse Coding**
2610.23801v1 by Jinho Choi, Hyesu Lim, Jaegul Choo, Steffen Schneider

Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) explain foundation model representations by decomposing activations into sparse combinations of learned dictionary atoms. However, the learned dictionaries can vary across training runs, limiting the reliability and reproducibility of SAE-based analyses. We propose Consistent Sparse Dictionary Learning (ConSDL), which replaces the standard linear encoder with per-sample sparse coding. This is motivated by our synthetic analysis showing that coefficients from a single linear encoder pass are suboptimal compared with those obtained by per-sample optimization, suggesting that sparse coding is a reliable mechanism for dictionary learning. Across 16 synthetic settings, ConSDL achieves near-perfect dictionary and coefficient recovery. These gains translate to state-of-the-art cross-seed consistency across four vision and language settings, where ConSDL improves dictionary and coefficient consistency by 17.6% and 35.9%, respectively, over the average of six SAE baselines.

摘要：

##### **DLite: History-free Speculative Decoding for Agentic AI**
2610.23787v1 by Jiaming Xu, Hanzhen Wang, Tianlang Zhao, Jiewen Xiao, Chengze Yuan, Jiayi Pan, Yongkang Zhou, Jiancai Ye, Yu Wang, Guohao Dai

Speculative decoding has become a mainstream technique for accelerating large language model (LLM) inference by using a lightweight draft model to generate candidate tokens that are subsequently verified by the target model. However, agentic workloads introduce long and continuously evolving contexts and frequent multi-turn interactions. Under such workloads, we observe that existing draft models suffer from up to an 80% degradation in acceptance length, while maintaining their historical KV cache introduces substantial system overhead. In this paper, we identify the growing historical context as a common source of both problems and introduce DLite, a history-free speculative decoding framework for agentic AI. DLite directly reuses contextual hidden states from the target model and performs local speculative prediction without explicitly maintaining the historical context, transforming an increasingly complex modeling problem into local simple prediction problem. A lightweight sequential head further restores intra-block causal dependencies with low overhead. To effectively train this heterogeneous history-free architecture, we introduce a tailored two-stage training strategy. Experiments on general-purpose, long-context and agentic workloads show that DLite achieves 2-6 acceptance length and outperforms the state-of-the-art speculative decoding method, DSpark, up to 70%.

摘要：

##### **VocalGrad: Evaluating Acoustic Perception in Audio Language Models**
2610.23745v1 by Koki Ryu, Hitomi Yanaka

Recent large audio language models (LALMs) have extended their capabilities beyond lexical speech understanding to tasks requiring paralinguistic and non-linguistic audio understanding, such as emotion recognition. However, these models still struggle to reliably utilize acoustic information, particularly when it co-occurs with spoken linguistic content. Existing benchmarks do not clearly distinguish acoustic perception from higher-level reasoning, making it difficult to identify the source of these failures. To address this issue, we introduce VocalGrad, a benchmark designed to evaluate LALMs' ability to perceive low-level acoustic variations. VocalGrad formulates this problem as a simple binary classification of temporal change direction, enabling evaluation largely independent of higher-level reasoning or semantic understanding. Our benchmark results reveal a substantial gap in acoustic perception between human annotators and state-of-the-art LALMs: while human annotators achieve high accuracy, LALMs perform at near-chance levels. We further conduct linear probing to identify structural bottlenecks in these models. Our analysis reveals two distinct failure modes: for some categories, task-relevant information is preserved in internal representations but not utilized by the language model head; for others, the information becomes progressively less linearly decodable across model layers. Overall, our results reveal substantial limitations of LALMs in modeling acoustic variation under controlled settings and expose representation differences not observable from text outputs.

摘要：

##### **The Italian lawmaking process: a unified and structured dataset**
2610.23741v1 by Matilde Contestabile, Chiara Ferrara, Giovanni Parrillo, Lorenzo Emer, Andrea Vandin

The Italian legislative process is documented in detailed parliamentary records hosted in institutional websites. However, much of this information is available only as unstructured text, limiting its systematic reuse. We applied a controlled, systematic LLM-based extraction procedure, validated against manually annotated records, to convert these texts into ordered and dated events including, e.g., bill introduction, committee examination, plenary examination, and approval. The resulting dataset reconstructs the procedural histories of all 5346 Italian laws enacted between 1985 and 2026, spanning parliamentary terms IX to XIX, for overall 101413 events. Each event records the activity, date, and chamber, with additional procedural details where available. The dataset supports research in the emerging fields of computational legal and legislative studies, potentially combined with process-oriented data science methods to examine procedural paths and legislative timing across parliamentary terms.

摘要：

##### **Bridging the Language Gap in Scholarly Data II: Enhancing Gender Inference for Chinese Names**
2610.23734v1 by Lisette Espín-Noboa, Mingrong She, Ana Maria Jaramillo, Liuhuaying Yang

Perceived gender inference from names is important for inclusive scientometrics research, yet existing methods perform poorly on non-Western scholars. We address this gap for Chinese names, which appear in two forms, Hanzi and Pinyin, its Romanized form (e.g., Zhang Wei, Li Na). In international bibliographic records, names are often available only in Pinyin. However, a single pinyin name can correspond to multiple distinct hanzi characters with different gender associations, so gender inference from Pinyin alone is intrinsically ambiguous. To address this, we first develop two hanzi models, a regression model that produces a continuous gender score from 0 (male) to 1 (female), and a classification model that outputs one of three labels (\em{male}, \em{neutral}, \em{female}). We train them on a sample of hanzi names from Chinese-language physics publications, rated by 11 native Chinese speakers. On this sample, the models achieve an RMSE of $0.09$ and an R$^2$ of $0.85$ (regression), and macro-AUC of $0.97$ and a Cohen's $κ$ of $0.81$ (classification), demonstrating high performance that is robust to class imbalance. For pinyin names, we propose a pipeline that aggregates hanzi predictions via transliteration and frequency weighting, together with an ambiguity score that flags names whose gender cannot be resolved from Pinyin alone. Benchmarked against three off-the-shelf tools (Namsor, GPT, and Claude), our models achieve the highest agreement with human annotations for both gender scores and gender labels, in both scripts. Applied to an independent sample of Chinese-named authors in APS journals, our models assign a gender to every name, whereas existing Pinyin-based methods leave between 20\% and 100\% of names unclassified. These findings demonstrate the importance of hanzi-level information for reliable gender inference of Chinese names. Code and data are publicly available.

摘要：

##### **Beyond Large Language Models: A Mental World Model Framework for Mental Modeling, Simulation, Evolution, and Interpretability**
2610.23722v1 by Qingshi Wang

Understanding and simulating how subjective cognition and mental states emerge, evolve, and shape behavior is essential for advancing artificial intelligence toward deeper social cognition and general intelligence. Existing large language models (LLMs) and Theory of Mind (ToM) methods primarily infer mental states from linguistic information, lacking unified mechanisms for subjective world construction, cognitive-psychological interactions, dynamic evolution, and historical dependencies. This paper introduces the Mental World Model (MWM) and its implementation, the Multi-Branch Mental Evolution Architecture (MMEA), which explicitly constructs subjective worlds, models interactions between cognition and mental states, and simulates continuous mental evolution shaped by historical experiences. On DynToM and RecToM, MMEA achieves 96.48% and 94.00% accuracy, respectively, outperforming leading LLMs and specialized ToM methods. Additional experiments demonstrate improvements of 28.7, 39.3, and 16.6 percentage points in mental state and behavior prediction, long-sequence prediction, and implicit mental state inference, respectively. The results demonstrate that MMEA provides an interpretable framework for continuous mental modeling, enabling AI to move beyond surface-level semantic representations to infer individuals' underlying mental states from linguistic information and dynamically simulate their internal cognitive processes and evolution over time. MWM also provides a promising foundation for causal reasoning, personalized AI, sustained human-AI interaction, and future advances toward artificial general intelligence (AGI).

摘要：

##### **Recognizing Study Lineage Is Not Enough: Over-Crediting Same-Trial Evidence in LLM-Based Medical Verification**
2610.23721v1 by Mukand Krishna, Anoushka Harit, Chenghao Xiao, Zhongtian Sun

Publication count is not study count. A model can identify shared trial lineage correctly in a direct task yet over-credit a second publication from the same trial when aggregating evidence. We evaluate this behavior in medical LLM verification using 14 source-audited clinical families and five model conditions. The families span provenance, clinical applicability, result direction, and composition; the provenance families provide the direct lineage tests. Recognition and aggregation are separate stateless calls, and repeated calls assess within-input stability. Across all three original provenance families, GPT-5.5 identifies the independent update in 10/10 recognition calls per family but over-credits the same-trial report in 5/5 separate aggregation calls per family. Selected Qwen3-4B and Ministral-3-8B conditions show the same cross-task contrast. A study-grouping instruction reduces GPT-5.5 over-credit from 4/5 to 0/5 in each later pure-provenance family. Other model conditions show mixed behavior, and rejected outputs limit several comparisons. The results identify a behavioral gap between elicited lineage recognition and evidence aggregation. The code and data artifacts are available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/study-lineage-review-artifact.

##### **Neural Expansion: A Unified Mechanism for How Deep Neural Networks Generalize**
2610.23716v1 by Chashi Mahiul Islam, Samuel Jacob Chacko, Mao Nishino, Canlin Zhang, Xiuwen Liu

Deep neural networks demonstrate remarkable generalization despite being highly over-parameterized, challenging traditional statistical learning theories. While several theoretical paradigms such as margin-based bounds, PAC-Bayesian analyses, and neural tangent kernels provide global explanations, they often lack mechanistic insight into how generalization emerges from training dynamics. We introduce Neural Expansion, a unified geometric framework that characterizes generalization through the expansion of directionally-selective ray coverage in the input space. Using Generalization Intervals (GIs), we quantify direction-specific regions around training samples where the model's predictions remain stable. Our extensive empirical studies across diverse architectures and datasets reveal that training leads to progressive expansion of ray coverage, particularly along directions with low Jacobian singular values. This mechanism helps explain a large fraction of correctly classified test inputs, adversarial examples, and even mislabeled or out-of-distribution samples through the geometry of ray coverage rather than instance-level memorization. By connecting input-space curvature to predictive behavior, our framework provides a mechanistic foundation that complements existing global theories of deep learning.

In psycholinguistics, reading time is commonly used as an indirect measure of cognitive processing effort. Building on this perspective, we investigate whether human-written texts and their LLM-generated rewrites exhibit differences in predicted reading time that may reflect differences in processing demands for readers. To this end, we introduce RoBERTa-RT, a model trained on human eye-tracking data to predict word-level reading times. We evaluate the model on both internal and external test sets, construct a paired dataset of 496 human-written articles and their LLM-generated rewrites, and apply RoBERTa-RT to compare their predicted reading times. Our findings show that LLM-generated rewrites tend to be shorter and have slightly lower predicted total reading times, while exhibiting higher predicted reading times per word. These differences are associated with systematic linguistic changes, including longer and less frequent words, greater lexical diversity, and higher contextual predictability. From a cognitive perspective, our findings suggest that LLM-generated writing may redistribute reading demands: reducing the amount of text to process while increasing the predicted processing effort associated with individual words. ##### **Optimist Deep Research: An Iterative, State-Centric Agent for Autonomous Literature Research**
2610.23299v1 by Gihan Panapitiya

Most retrieval-augmented systems treat literature search as a single retrieval step followed by summarization. Real literature research works differently: the investigator's understanding of the question changes as they read, new subquestions appear, and evidence is weighed, not just collected. We present Optimist Deep Research, an open-source agent that runs literature research as an iterative evidence-acquisition process over an explicit, mutable research state. A research question is broken into a tree of subquestions, each served by search intents that are translated into source-specific queries over arXiv, OpenAlex, Crossref, PubMed, Semantic Scholar and the web. Results are merged into a canonical, provenance-preserving document registry through a conservative four-tier deduplication hierarchy. The registry is expanded by citation snowballing until a saturation criterion is met, and then screened, ranked and allocated to subquestions round-robin. Documents are read in parallel into a structured evidence store of claims with support, location, type and strength. A critic reviews the state along eight dimensions and returns research actions rather than a binary verdict: it can add, split, rephrase, deprioritize or close subquestions, so the research plan is an output of the investigation as well as an input. The final report is written section by section from the evidence store, and every citation resolves to a retrieved document. We describe the design, the deterministic machinery that keeps a language-model pipeline auditable, and a case study that read 28 of 237 retrieved documents in about three and a half minutes. We also report weaknesses the case study exposed.

##### **FlashReFocus: Interactive Image Refocusing in Seconds**
2610.23297v1 by Ching-Heng Cheng, Chia-Ming Lee, Ming-Ching Chang, Xin Li, Yu-Lun Liu, Chih-Chung Hsu

Single-image refocusing changes the focal plane and depth of field of a photograph after capture. It is inherently interactive: users try many focal points and blur strengths on one image, so each edit should be fast and change only the blur. Recent generative methods, however, rerun large diffusion models for every edit. We observe that the two sub-problems differ in kind: recovering the all-in-focus image is ill-posed and needs a generative prior, whereas rendering blur is largely determined by depth and the focal plane. We therefore propose FlashReFocus, which runs diffusion once per photograph and renders every edit with a lightweight network. To address the ill-posed restoration, a one-step diffusion deblurrer with a decoder refiner recovers fine detail efficiently while balancing fidelity and perceptual quality. For precise control, the renderer is conditioned on an explicit defocus map and uses defocus-bias attention, which lets pixels at similar defocus share information without interference from pixels at other depths; in-focus content stays unchanged, and editing the map yields tilt-shift and two focal planes. To further test generalization to unseen cameras, we collect 3CReal, a three-camera benchmark of 89 pairs. FlashReFocus achieves the best perceptual fidelity for zero-shot bokeh rendering on EBB! and 3CReal, refocuses 148.3x faster than GenRefocus, and takes about half a second per further edit, 462x faster.

##### **CoTA++: Understanding the Repeat Curse in dMLLMs from an Information Flow Perspective**
2610.23290v1 by Qiyan Zhao, Xiaofeng Zhang, Xu-Yao Zhang, Shijie Han, Zhepeng Li, Shunzhi Zhu, Da-Han Wang

Diffusion-based multimodal large language models (dMLLMs) decode by iterative denoising with bidirectional attention, which precludes the standard autoregressive key--value cache. Practical deployments therefore rely on approximate caching that reuses intermediate states across denoising steps. Such caching, however, often makes a response repeat one token at adjacent positions in a run that breaks the sentence, a failure we term the \emph{Repeat Curse}. Tracing the context-token information flow reveals three disruptions. Within a step, caching disperses the attention that anchors a position on nearby context tokens. Across steps, similarity-based refresh is not staleness-aware, so some positions are decoded against stale key--value states. Across layers, the deep-layer entropy of the context fails to converge where repetition arises. We accordingly propose CoTA++, a training-free, plug-and-play framework that counteracts each disruption where it originates: Context-Token Attention Re-anchoring re-forms the attention routing against the current context, Decode-Aware Refresh reserves part of the cache's refresh budget for the positions about to be committed, and Context-Token Entropy-Guided Voting discounts the confidence of a position by the deep-layer entropy of its context, which reorders the unmasking. The three components act at non-overlapping stages of a decoding step and compose freely with existing dMLLMs and caches. Across three dMLLMs and two caching backends, CoTA++ reduces the sample repetition rate of long responses from up to 99.6\% to at most 17.0\%, recovers most of the accuracy lost to caching on nine benchmarks, and keeps the speedup of caching at under 15\% overhead, making approximate caching reliable for dMLLMs.

##### **Annotrace: A Workbench for Step-Level Annotation of Agent Traces**
2610.23284v1 by Arun Josephraj Arokiaraj, Chenyi Huang, Sahan Bulathwela, Zekun Wu, Bin Wu

Annotrace reads six trace formats, including OpenTelemetry GenAI, LangSmith and Langfuse, and turns each run into an ordered, nested list of steps. The annotator writes their own labelling codebook; a frontier model proposes a starting set from a sample of their own traces, answers first, and is asked exactly the questions the human is asked, making annotation a joint human-AI process. ##### **Sample, Mix, or Verify? Allocating Test-Time Compute under Imperfect Verifiers**
2610.23255v1 by Srinivasan Iyengar

A common way to make language models and agents more accurate is to spend more compute at inference time: generate many candidate solutions and submit the one a verifier prefers. Success then requires that some candidate is correct and that the verifier picks it. The first grows predictably with the number of samples; the second does not, because real verifiers (unit tests, majority votes, LLM judges, reward models) accept wrong answers, reject right ones, and make correlated mistakes. With a fixed budget, a practitioner must decide how many samples to draw, which models to draw them from, and how much to spend on verification, and today these choices are made by rules of thumb. We introduce SPLIT, which makes them from a small labelled pilot. It rests on an exact formula for the probability of submitting a correct answer under accept/reject and top-score selection that accounts for varying task difficulty, blind spots shared across models, and correlated repeated verifier calls. SPLIT fits the formula by empirical Bayes and uses it to plan budgets on unseen tasks. Across seven benchmarks spanning math, science QA, code and software-engineering agents, 26 models, 175 agent systems and six kinds of real verifiers, all from public releases, SPLIT lands within 0.02-0.8 percentage points of the best allocation in hindsight, while every fixed rule we test loses at least 2.5 points in some setting. Regret depends only on how well SPLIT ranks configurations, and it stays near-optimal under compute, price and latency costs and online. ##### **Think More or Read More? A Token-Budget Theory of Reasoning with Shared Context**

We construct a 6,536-sentence Bai-Chinese corpus and evaluate on a 1,536-sentence test set. MAS-Bai achieves 16.23 BLEU and 62.15 COMET, improving over Standard RAG by 8.11 BLEU and 6.85 COMET. Forward LoRA, expert ablations and controlled routing experiments distinguish the roles of supervised adaptation, Bai-specific expertise and conditional routing. The largest gains occur on Bowen phonetic loans and divergent Bai word order, while human evaluation and reverse-verifier analyses support the automatic results.

##### **Validating LLM-generated political networks with survey-reported partisan exposure**
2610.23199v1 by Marco Tonin, Michele Tizzoni, Bruno Lepri, Luca Maria Aiello

Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to simulate human behavior in social science research, including decisions, interactions, and social relationships. However, in network formation tasks, existing evaluations often focus on aggregate network properties, while networks with plausible structural properties may still fail to reproduce individuals' social and political environments. Here, we generate LLM-based political networks using personas sampled from the nationally representative ANES 2020--2022 Social Media Study and use respondents' survey-reported partisan exposure as an empirical benchmark for the political environments generated for the corresponding personas. We first characterize the structural properties and political assortativity of the generated networks, and then compare ego-level partisan exposure with the survey. We find that the structure and political assortativity of the generated networks vary across experimental configurations. More importantly, when comparing generated partisan exposure with the survey, some models generate stronger separation between Democratic and Republican egos than reported in the survey, with higher same-party and lower cross-party exposure, while others mainly underestimate Republican exposure across ego groups. Moreover, networks with similar political assortativity can still generate different partisan environments around the same personas, including among networks with plausible structural properties. Finally, generated networks show group-specific errors, under- or overestimating exposure to either Democrats or Republicans for specific social groups. These findings show that evaluating LLM-based social simulations only through aggregate outcomes can hide errors in the local environments through which those outcomes emerge, highlighting the importance of validation at both aggregate and individual levels.

##### **Framing Spatial Hearing within the Free Energy Principle: A Hierarchical Model of Perceptual Learning**
2610.23196v1 by Roberto Barumerli, Nils Meyer-Kahlen, Lorenzo Picinali

Spatial hearing relies on dynamic internal representations to decode spatial information from ever-changing acoustic conditions. A fundamental example is sound localisation, where listeners infer the direction of an invisible sound. Bayesian inference provides a computational account of such a localisation process; however, modelling efforts are largely limited to static listening scenarios. Instead, the free energy principle (FEP) describes perception, learning, and action as joint minimisation of variational free energy (VFE), providing a principled framework to account for dynamic conditions. Here, we take a first step toward applying the FEP to spatial hearing. We characterise how listeners' beliefs evolve across trials, in which they estimated sound direction and received subsequent visual feedback on each trial. Listeners are described by a hierarchical generative model where visual feedback drives gradient descent on VFE, updating beliefs over three independent quantities: lateral and polar localisation precisions and hemifield disambiguation. The model captures individual learning trajectories and reveals components with different dynamics: lateral precision improves, as does hemifield disambiguation, consistent with increased sensitivity of the auditory pathway in decoding vertical direction. Notably, polar precision also increases, most for listeners with the lowest initial precision, suggesting that the observed vertical improvement is driven by both hemifield disambiguation and polar precision. This work establishes spatial hearing as a tractable domain for FEP and motivates future closed-loop experiments with full action-perception cycles.

##### **FuzzInsight: Navigating the Efficiency-Effectiveness Trade-off in Coverage-Guided DNN Fuzzing**
2610.23195v1 by Vivek Vekariya, Fathiyeh Faghih, Alexander Pretschner

Collecting and labeling diverse test datasets for evaluating Deep Neural Networks (DNNs) is costly and time-consuming. This has motivated coverage-guided fuzzing techniques that generate defect-revealing corner cases from limited test data. However, their effectiveness depends heavily on configuration choices, particularly coverage criteria and seed-selection strategies. We present a configuration-oriented analysis and decision guidelines for scalable DNN fuzzing targeting two bottlenecks: inefficient seed utilization and the cost of coverage guidance. Our analysis shows that existing seed-selection strategies repeatedly prioritize a small subset of inputs, leaving many collected seeds unexplored. To address this issue, we propose Equalized Sampling, a constraint-based strategy that increases mean seed queue utilization to 77%, improving on prior approaches by 13 percentage points.
  We further evaluate 84 fuzzing configurations on four benchmark datasets: MNIST, SVHN, GTSRB, and CIFAR-100. Results show that the non-structural criteria LSCD, LSC, and MDSC achieve coverage outcomes comparable to structural and alternative non-structural criteria while requiring substantially less computation. These criteria also promote broad behavioral exploration of the DNN under test, reducing the need to monitor multiple coverage metrics. Among them, LSCD provides a modest but consistent runtime advantage.
  Finally, we show that retraining with generated corner cases improves generalization robustness by up to 77 percentage points on average without degrading performance on the original test distribution. Together, these findings provide a practical blueprint for selecting fuzzing configurations that balance efficiency, behavioral exploration, and model hardening.

##### **OfficeTown: Generative Organizations for Evolving Agent Intelligence**
2610.23194v1 by Hongchen Wei, Daohong Ying, Zhi Hong, Aidan Wu, Bei Liu, Ruichun Ma, Yifan Yang, Qi Dai, Kai Qiu, Rui Xu, Dongdong Chen, Zhenzhong Chen, Chong Luo

AI agents increasingly use files and software tools to produce professional deliverables. Such work draws on materials, decisions, and feedback accumulated across assignments. We introduce OfficeTown, a generative simulation framework for professional organizations in which tasks and their context arise from continuing organizational work. From a natural-language description of an organization's purpose and activities, OfficeTown initializes an organization whose members coordinate work, delegate bounded assignments to assistant agents, and review their deliverables. Artifacts, decisions, and unresolved matters persist and shape subsequent work, while responsibilities and permissions determine what each member can see and do. We curate work requests, their context, and review feedback into executable resources for evaluation and learning. We construct OfficeTown Bench with 406 tasks and 4,836 evaluation criteria from 23 simulated organizations across eight professional domains, incorporating domain-expert feedback. Frontier-model evaluations reveal substantial remaining headroom. Supervised fine-tuning and on-policy distillation on OfficeTown data substantially improve Qwen3.5-9B on external professional-work benchmarks, including GDPval. Supplementary evaluations show that the principal--assistant harness achieves higher scores than the single- and multi-agent baselines tested. These results highlight the value of organizational simulation in generating work-grounded data and environments for the continued development of generalist agents.

##### **LangNCO: Compiling Natural-Language Constraints into Inference-Time Interventions for Neural Combinatorial Optimization**
2610.23190v1 by Hironori Ohigashi

Neural combinatorial optimization (NCO) rapidly produces high-quality solutions for problem structures encountered during training, but operational requirements may change after deployment and be communicated in natural language. We propose LangNCO, a framework that compiles instance-specific requirements into structured inference-time interventions for a frozen NCO solver, without changing the pretrained policy's architecture, learned input representation, or parameters. A pretrained LLM serves as a replaceable semantic front end, translating each requirement into a schema-validated domain-specific language (DSL) rule set. A deterministic Rule Executor applies the rules as nonexpansive hard action masks. Because local masking prevents immediate violations but does not ensure that a complete feasible continuation remains reachable, Critical-Offset Decoding extracts rule-referenced critical nodes and reallocates part of a fixed rollout budget to multi-strength biased rollouts. Using controlled task-specific language to isolate solver adaptation from open-ended language robustness, we evaluate a CVRP policy on road closures, time windows, vehicle assignments, visit-order requirements, all pairwise combinations, and twelve additional constraint types. LangNCO achieves 100% feasibility on the four primary datasets with mean reference gaps of 1.63--3.26%, 97.33--100% feasibility when separately generated rules are composed for pairwise constraints, and 98.67% feasibility across the additional constraints. These results demonstrate modular post-training adaptation of a frozen NCO solver through inspectable language-derived rules, without retraining.

##### **A Stronger Teacher Within: Strengthening MoE Models via On-Policy Bidirectional Distillation**
2610.23185v1 by Haoyu Chen, Jun Bai, Yang Liu, Jianfei Zhang, Wenge Rong

Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has substantially improved Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) LLMs, but its outcome-level rewards provide only sparse supervision. On-Policy Distillation offers dense token-level feedback, yet requires a stronger compatible teacher, while on-policy self-distillation relies on hand-designed privileged information. We observe that the MoE architecture itself offers a way to resolve this dilemma, as increasing the activation budget per token exposes latent expert capacity and yields a potentially stronger teacher within the model. However, naive expansion degrades performance because newly co-activated experts are not optimized to work together. We therefore propose MoEvolve, a bidirectional distillation framework following a recovery-then-improvement strategy: we first restore the activation-expanded high-$k$ model and further strengthen it by exploiting its additional active capacity, then reverse the direction of distillation to transfer the enhanced capabilities back into the original low-$k$ configuration, retaining its inference-time activation budget. Across diverse MoE models and benchmarks, MoEvolve improves the initial models by up to $22.2$ domain-average points and outperforms same-budget RLVR baselines by up to $6.5$ points. Notably, the final low-$k$ models often surpass their activation-expanded teachers.

##### **What LLMs Do in the Shadows: Reading Context Reliance from the Unembedding Dark Space**
2610.23182v1 by Alexandra Kuleshova, Andrey Volodichev, Daria Kotova, Alexey Zaytsev

Detecting faithfulness hallucinations in large language models (LLMs) is crucial for their safe deployment in high-stakes domains. Recent approaches address this by contrasting contextual against parametric knowledge utilization, yet measuring context reliance typically demands noticeable overhead, such as materializing attention maps, running additional forward passes, or training auxiliary probes. In this work, we show that context reliance can be estimated by projecting hidden states onto the dark space - directions that compose weakly with the unembedding matrix. Beyond its recognized function as an initial-token attention sink, we show that this subspace actively mediates context processing, and that its share of hidden-state norm - termed the Dark Matter Ratio (DMR) - provides a direct score of context utilization. Pairing DMR with a parametric knowledge score yields DarkLens, a lightweight faithfulness detector operating strictly within a single forward pass. Evaluated across three QA and summarization benchmarks on six open-weight LLMs (4B-13B parameters), DarkLens matches or outperforms a wide range of baselines without requiring extra forward passes or probe training.

##### **Tree-Aware Posterior Distillation for Long-Horizon Agentic Reinforcement Learning**
2610.23181v1 by Heming Zou, Qi Wang, Yixiu Mao, Yun Qu, Yuyi Guo, Zeyuan Liu, Chenglin Li, Weijie Liu, Saiyong Yang, Xiangyang Ji

Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards has become a scalable post-training paradigm for large language models. However, training agents over long-horizon interactions presents two challenges: using only terminal rewards makes turn-level credit assignment sparse and high-variance, while sequential mini-batch updates on a fixed rollout batch introduce policy staleness as the current policy drifts from the behavior policy. Existing approaches obtain denser credit via learned critics, process reward models, or on-policy distillation, but they rely on a value network, step-level annotations, or a stronger external teacher that increase training complexity. Meanwhile, importance sampling and clipping mitigate policy drift, but they do not specify a finite target that stops sequential updates from overshooting. In this work, we introduce TreeOPSD, a critic-free, tree-aware posterior self-distillation framework for long-horizon agentic reinforcement learning. Treating each interaction turn as a tree node, TreeOPSD branches alternative continuations from shared prefixes and recursively backs up terminal rewards into implicit turn-level credit. It then maps each credit to a closed-form, bounded teacher distribution parameterized by the likelihood ratio relative to the frozen behavior policy. Minimizing the divergence between this teacher and the student policy recovers the policy-gradient direction as the tangent at the behavior policy while bounding updates toward a finite target across sequential mini-batches. Empirically, TreeOPSD improves agentic capabilities across several competitive benchmarks and model scales, especially for long-horizon tasks, e.g., raising the average accuracy of Qwen3-30B-A3B-Thinking-2507 by 4.97 points over GRPO on the Deep Research setting.

##### **Do Speech BCIs Need Larger Models? Rethinking Neural Decoding beyond Scaling**
2610.23175v1 by Zehui Feng, Weichuan Wang, Xiaohan Chen, Cuntai Guan, Ting Han

Recent speech brain-computer interfaces (BCIs) increasingly rely on large models and complex multi-stage pipelines for neural decoding. However, in speech BCI, where training data are scarce, noisy, and expensive to collect, the benefits of model scaling remain unclear. This work revisits a fundamental question: are large models truly necessary for accurate neural decoding? We propose BEST (Brain-Encoding-Speech-Text), a two-stage framework for efficient neural-to-text decoding. Instead of relying on large generative models or phoneme-centric pipelines, BEST introduces hierarchical intermediate representations and a compact speech-oriented decoder that directly maps neural activity to text. This design preserves essential structure while significantly reducing computational cost, enabling robust generalization in low-resource settings and practical deployment under constrained resources. On the Brain-to-Text '24 and '25 benchmarks, BEST achieves word error rates (WERs) of 6.19% and 1.62%, respectively, using only 3.85% of the parameters required by comparable methods. Beyond these results, we provide three key insights: (1) decoder scaling yields non-monotonic gains; (2) cross-modal alignment is more critical than model size; and (3) overfitting degards sequence-level performance. Representation analysis further reveals a consistent neural-audio-text alignment pathway.

##### **Chinese Decision: A Family of Language Models for Single-Pass Probabilistic Decision-Making**
2610.23170v1 by Qiang Yan

Many language-based applications require probabilities over a finite set of decisions rather than open-ended responses. We present Chinese Decision, a family of Chinese-language models for single-pass probabilistic decision-making. Given context, an instruction, and candidate descriptions, the model extracts final-position candidate-letter logits in one forward pass and normalizes them over valid options, without autoregressive answer generation. A shared interface supports Boolean judgments, categorical choices, and ordinal scores. We report a completed 0.8B model trained with distribution cross-entropy on 13.048 million decisions and preliminary 2B checkpoints trained with an additional RLCD objective. On the full CJ-Bench of 307,900 decisions, the uncalibrated 0.8B model achieves 72.24% general-domain accuracy and 76.98% average accuracy across medicine, law, and finance using a single checkpoint, with 1.07% overall expected calibration error. The 2B model reaches 72.56% general-domain accuracy after 40.13% of one epoch but has not surpassed the completed 0.8B model on the professional-domain average. We further evaluate a validation-temperature-scaled deployment model and its Q8 quantization, finding close aggregate performance while documenting individual prediction changes. We release the training, evaluation, and deployment pipeline. The current results establish a reproducible approach to Chinese probabilistic decisions but do not isolate the effect of model scale.

##### **Explaining Chess and Go in Natural Language**
2610.23166v1 by Ori Press, Alexander Golubev

Modern chess engines play far beyond human ability, often choosing moves whose underlying ideas are difficult for humans to understand. We propose an automatic metric for evaluating chess explanations, grounded in chess-engine evaluations. Evaluating state-of-the-art language models using this metric, we find that they perform surprisingly poorly, often relying on surface-level board observations rather than robust, transferable chess concepts. We then introduce BinProp, a training-free method that enables these same models to discover reusable chess concepts and use them to explain moves in natural language. BinProp improves explainability for every model tested, nearly tripling the average score compared with direct prompting. Beyond chess, the same method discovers useful concepts in Go, draughts, and shogi without additional tuning.

##### **Algorithm Modelization: Transforming Optimization Algorithms into Evolvable Intelligence**
2610.23161v1 by Jian Yao, Zhiyao Zhang, Yichen Li, Xingyu Wu, Kay Chen Tan

Can algorithms be learned and evolved as models, rather than repeatedly redesigned as programs? We explore this question by introducing Algorithm Modelization, a new paradigm that turns iterative algorithms into trainable models. Its central idea is to regard algorithm execution trajectories as computational experience: by learning how algorithmic states evolve over iterations, a model can internalize the knowledge embodied in existing algorithms, reproduce their computational behaviors, and further evolve beyond them through training. As a first realization of this paradigm, we modelize iterative optimization as an autoregressive process, where preceding generations constitute the computational context for generating the next population. We develop a dimension-independent population-temporal architecture to capture both population interactions and iterative dynamics, together with an imitation-to-surpassing learning framework that first acquires algorithmic knowledge from heterogeneous teachers and then improves the resulting optimizer directly through optimization feedback. The modelized optimizer consequently goes beyond reproducing individual algorithms: it consolidates heterogeneous search knowledge into a single trainable model, achieves stronger optimization capability through subsequent improvement, and generalizes its acquired knowledge across problems and substantially larger unseen dimensions. More fundamentally, Algorithm Modelization makes algorithmic capability accumulable through learning, where new algorithms and problem-solving experience can become training resources for progressively improving the model. Our results establish a promising path from manually designed algorithms toward general algorithm models that learn, generalize, and evolve from computational experience.

Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as computational models of human behavior. We train Cross-Centaur to predict a participant's target-task actions from a target-task description and the source-task description paired with the same participant's source-task behavior. Probing analyses suggest that its hidden states encode participant-level behavioral tendencies that transfer across tasks. These results highlight the potential of cross-task individual-level data for predicting human decision-making with language models. ##### **Can AI interview? Comparing AI-led to Human-led Chat Interviews**
2610.23149v1 by Semra Guven, Tobias Gårdhus, Andreas Bjerre-Nielsen, Anna Rogers, Hjalmar Bang Carlsen

Large language model chatbots have been proposed as AI-interviewers for collecting qualitative interview data at scale, potentially bridging social science's longstanding divide between the breadth of surveys and the depth of qualitative interviews. This paper provides the first direct empirical comparison of data quality in AI-led and human-led interviews. In a randomized experiment (N = 41), university students were interviewed via synchronous text chat by either trained non-expert interviewers or a locally hosted AI system; we evaluate exposure, specificity, and relevance. Median interview duration was similar across conditions, but AI-interviewers asked more than twice as many questions per interview and elicited roughly 65 percent more respondent text per interview. Per-question responses were longer with human interviewers, though not after multiple-testing correction; only AI-condition participants skipped questions. Responses were almost uniformly on-topic in both conditions; for specificity we detect no difference, but the design cannot exclude one of up to three quarters of a point. These results were obtained with a small, locally hosted open-weight model (7B parameters), which addresses concerns about data security, GDPR compliance, and scientific reproducibility. Our findings support the viability of AI-interviewing for large-N qualitative data collection.

##### **Local proxies for memory-efficient training**
2610.23145v1 by Wu S. Zihan, Brian A. Yuan, Guillaume Bellec

Local learning algorithms can reshape resource requirements of artificial intelligence. The memory bottleneck of standard end-to-end backpropagation lies in the need to store all network activations until the loss gradient has been propagated back through. Local learning avoids this, but typically at a cost in accuracy. We study how to achieve accurate and memory efficient local learning in transformers. For each transformer block, we train a local proxy network to predict the global network output via distillation. Gradients can be independently propagated through this local proxy while estimating well the backpropagation gradients. In theory, this approach is exact for deep linear networks; in non-linear residual models, the gradient error can be bounded when using the appropriate residual proxy. We verify this numerically and demonstrate that optimizing the distillation loss improves gradient estimation. Evaluating the performance of proxy propagation on CIFAR-100 in vision transformers, we find that it achieves a better accuracy/memory trade-off compared to other local algorithms. Finally, we show that the method is further relevant for fine-tuning large language models on a small memory budget. Our method trains faster and achieves higher performance than memory-efficient zeroth-order learning algorithms with comparable memory cost.

Large Language Models (LLMs) have greatly assisted legal professionals. To better assist laypeople, legal LLMs must identify focal points of dispute and proactively elicit crucial context from users. Unfortunately, existing data resources often focus on interaction and response quality, offering limited insight into how proactive interaction affects LLMs' downstream claim-level judgment prediction in actual civil cases. To address this gap, we introduce \textbf{LexPilot}, a benchmark linking proactive legal interaction to claim-level verdict prediction using authentic Chinese civil judgments. Experiments reveal that current LLMs struggle with proactive legal interactions in underspecified consultations, with downstream judgment prediction substantially underperforming that in the fully specified consultation setup (-23.84% on average).
