#### Abstracts
##### **GLOBE: Evolving Structured Agentic Memory for E-commerce Product Optimization in Generative Search**
2610.23709v1 by Hyunseo Kim, Suil Choe, Sukkyoung So, Daeyeon Yi

Optimizing product listings for generative search requires language agents not only to rewrite effectively but also to continually self-improve by retaining successful strategies across diverse categories. However, existing approaches often treat structured listings as free-form text, leaving effective strategies implicit in prompts, search trajectories, or rewritten outputs rather than accumulating them as a reusable agentic memory. To address this limitation, we introduce GLOBE, an agentic framework that drives continual self-improvement by storing optimization knowledge in an evolving handbook of explicit, field-scoped behaviors. In each episode, an Editor applies relevant behaviors and receives rank-based feedback, which a Reflector converts into behavior updates. Crucially for dependable execution in real-world settings, a Moderator removes redundant proposals, a Curator commits accepted changes, and consistently harmful behaviors are deactivated. This cycle transforms episodic feedback into a persistent, field-scoped optimization memory. Experiments show that structured behaviors outperform monolithic prompt optimization, while feedback-driven evolution and moderation further improve effectiveness and reduce downside risk. The learned behaviors also remain effective on unseen product categories, suggesting that agentic memory evolution supports interpretable and transferable product optimization.

摘要：

##### **GEMS: Molecular Structure Elucidation as Learned Search over Isomer Graphs**
2610.23617v1 by Utku Umur Acikalin, Yingheng Wang, Goncalo J. Gouveia, Tyler J. Schwertfeger, Frank C. Schroeder, Carla P. Gomes

Identifying small-molecule structures from molecular formulas and tandem mass spectra remains a bottleneck in untargeted metabolomics and natural-product discovery. Even when database retrieval or de novo prediction misses the exact structure, the resulting candidates can provide starting points for exploring nearby structures through a few bond edits. We introduce GEMS (Guided Editing of Molecular Structures), which refines candidates through learned 2-switch bond edits that preserve the molecular formula and each atom's total valence. GEMS uses MIST, a pretrained spectrum-to-fingerprint model, to predict fingerprint probabilities once per spectrum. A neural policy compares each candidate's fingerprint with these predictions, links present features to the atoms and bonds that generate them, and scores all valid edits in one forward pass. The policy is trained on shortest edit paths between structures, so it can be pretrained on PubChem isomers without spectra and then fine-tuned with spectrum-predicted fingerprints. At inference, GEMS searches from multiple initial candidates matching the supplied molecular formula and ranks candidates by fingerprint agreement and multi-start consensus. With the target structure excluded from PubChem initialization, three edit rounds achieve Hit@1/Hit@10 of 29.64%/50.81% on NPLIB1 and 30.22%/41.33% on MassSpecGym, exceeding published baselines under comparable settings. On NPLIB1, GEMS achieves higher identification accuracy in about 3.3 minutes end to end, compared with 26.2 hours for our FRIGID run. GEMS also improves identification from generated candidates, including those refined by FRIGID. Experiments with exact target fingerprints identify fingerprint prediction error as a bottleneck in target recovery and final ranking. Deeper search recovers additional targets without retraining, with larger gains from weaker initial candidates.

摘要：

##### **From Trajectories to Differentiated Skills: Cognitive Differentiation for Self-Improving Agents**
2610.23599v1 by Yewei Zhao, Derong Xu, Wei Chen, Yin Xu, Peilun Zhou, Xiaohan Wang, Guojun Yin, Wei Lin, Mingjun Xiao

Large language model (LLM) agents tackle complex tasks through reasoning and interaction with external environments. Agent skills provide persistent, transferable capabilities that guide task planning, inform contextual decisions, support tool use, and enable executable procedures. Yet existing skill-evolution methods largely organize knowledge around skill artifacts, overlooking the functional heterogeneity within individual skills and its implications for knowledge maintenance and use. Inspired by cognitive science, we propose Cognitive Differentiation in Skill Evolution (CDSE), which evolves a unified skill through four functionally distinct yet coordinated components: Meta, Experience, Tool, and Script. CDSE extracts evidence-grounded semantic gradients from successful and failed trajectories, consolidates them by functional role, and applies component-specific updates and validation with cross-component coordination and adaptive invocation. An analysis of 500 top-ranked skill packages shows that 96.8% contain knowledge spanning at least two functional roles. Across six benchmarks, three agent models, and two execution harnesses, CDSE achieves the highest scores in all 36 settings, improving over the Base agent by 7.6 percentage points and the strongest competing method in each setting by 2.6 points on average. Ablation studies further support the importance of functional differentiation and adaptive invocation.

摘要：

##### **Darwin-Claw: Environment-Adaptive Experience Consolidation for Recursively Self-Evolving Agents**
2610.23574v1 by Mingze Yin, Xiaohan Wang, Dian Li, Longfei Luo, Jiahong Yan, Yiheng Zhu, Chang-Yu Hsieh

While Large Language Model (LLM) agents have demonstrated remarkable capabilities across complex tasks, they predominantly operate in isolated sessions, lacking the ability to continually learn from historical interactions or adapt to user-specific environments. Existing self-evolution paradigms often indiscriminately inject raw interaction trajectories into memory or skill modules, leading to obscured behavioral patterns and compromised update controllability. Furthermore, as foundational LLM reasoning improves, the bottleneck for agentic evolution shifts from accumulating generic knowledge to effectively managing situational experience. To address this, we propose Darwin-Claw, an environment-adaptive self-evolving framework that systematically distills episodic experiences to drive the comprehensive evolution of operational skills and durable memory. Theoretically grounded in an iterative agent-actor and evolver-critic co-adaptation, Darwin-Claw introduces a Hierarchical Experience Consolidation System that extracts fine-grained error attributions and generalizes them into dynamically updated prototype vectors. This structured situational experience serves as interaction patches for context-sensitive perception and explicit optimization signals for skill refinement. Practically, Darwin-Claw is a readily deployable, automated framework compatible with mainstream agent systems without requiring manual curation. Extensive experiments on WildClawBench demonstrate that Darwin-Claw yields substantial performance improvements, establishing a controllable and stable pathway for the continual, user-centric adaptation of autonomous agents in real-world ecosystems.

摘要：

##### **ConvexLLM: Scaling Convex-Hull Speech Interfaces to General-Purpose Audio Intelligence**
2610.23539v1 by Ming-Hao Hsu, Shujie Liu, Yuxuan Hu, Yuxiang Wang, Yingda Shen, Jinyu Li, Zhizheng Wu

Speech and audio language models connect continuous acoustic representations to decoders pretrained primarily on text data. Most such models use MLP or Q-Former interfaces to map acoustic features into the decoder's input space, requiring the decoder to learn how to interpret the resulting representations. We investigate whether grounding these representations in the decoder's existing text embeddings can help it use acoustic information while better preserving its pretrained text capabilities. We present ConvexLLM, a general-purpose speech and audio language model whose C-Gate interface represents audio as continuous convex combinations of frozen text embeddings. ConvexLLM achieves an eleven-task VoiceBench aggregate of 75.36, the highest among the open-weight models we compare, and an MMAU Test-mini accuracy of 62.50%. Its largest margins over the baselines are on spoken knowledge and reasoning tasks, while the strongest baselines are more accurate on audio understanding and transcription. In a comparison with a 2B-parameter decoder, C-Gate achieves higher aggregate spoken-language performance than MLP and Q-Former interfaces, with text-task performance close to MLP and task-dependent tradeoffs in transcription and audio understanding. These results support convex combinations of text embeddings as a viable interface for general-purpose speech and audio language models.

摘要：

##### **Truth in Contrast: Calibrated Factual Evidence from Language Model Likelihoods**
2610.23527v1 by Ponhvoan Srey, Jason Lucas, Pittawat Taveekitworachai, Junyoung Lee, Anh Tuan Luu

Large Language Models (LLMs) encode substantial factual knowledge. Yet, estimating confidence in factual claims reliably remains challenging. We study likelihood-based factual confidence from the complementary perspectives of discrimination and calibration across multilingual factual statements. Specifically, we instantiate contrastive generative likelihoods as Contrastive Factual Evidence (CFE), which compares the likelihood of the same claim under competing factual and non-factual conditions, and derive its probabilistic counterpart, the Contrastive Factual Posterior (CFP). We show analytically that CFP calibration depends on the differential error between the two surrogate conditionals, allowing errors shared across them to cancel and yield tighter calibration bounds. Empirically, CFP substantially improves calibration while maintaining factual discrimination comparable to its strongest competitor. Nonetheless, discrimination is uniformly weak across methods in lower-resource languages, which simultaneously undermines the utility of the measured calibration. To this end, we interpret a likelihood geometry, within which an evidence-bearing direction is induced, along with an orthogonal evidence-invariant direction. In our investigation, we selectively align target-language claims with stronger English evidence only along the factual-evidence direction, improving discrimination and open-ended factual generation without target-language labels.

摘要：

##### **MuninAI: A Self-Hosted Agentic Framework for Independent Research Groups**
2610.23480v1 by Markus Fischer, Jens Meiler, Matthias Elgeti

Research groups that want a language model grounded in their own literature face a choice between hosted services that receive their unpublished work and self-hosted deployments that keep the data local but give the model no access to the corpus. Munin is an open-source framework for a self-hosted scientific assistant that indexes a group's corpus and its citation graph, and wraps whichever open-weight model the group can serve in an agent harness with specialised reading, search, and computation tools. We claim that the harness, rather than the specific model, is what makes the system useful on domain literature, and we ship the evaluation suite that tests this claim with the software.
  On 199 paired literature questions, the harness more than doubles the accuracy of the base model, from 0.387 to 0.874 (+0.487, 95% CI [0.407, 0.568]), while naive retrieve-then-answer scores below the unaided model on every backbone tested. Under the harness, roughly twice as many answer claims trace to a passage the model read itself as with fixed top-5 retrieval. When the source paper for a question is removed from the corpus, the system abstains on about nine in ten of the questions it had answered correctly on the Qwen backbones and eight in ten on gpt-oss-20b, and it produces no fabricated local citation across 300 fabricated references.
  The pattern holds on three backbones from two developers, including a 20-billion-parameter model that runs on a single consumer-grade GPU. With all outbound network access disabled, both Qwen backbones still exceed the published PaperQA2 accuracy. The evaluation suite runs as a single command, so an adopting group can re-run it on its own corpus and model.

摘要：

##### **S-GAP: Stealthy Graph Attack Primitives for Graph Large Language Models**
2610.23465v1 by Wenda Li, Tongya Zheng, Shunyu Liu, Hanyang Yuan, Kaixuan Chen, Yu Wang, Yuchen Ying, Haoze Jiang, Huan Li, Gang Chen, Zujie Ren

Graph Large Language Models (GraphLLMs) enable powerful semantic understanding and reasoning over Text-Attributed Graphs (TAGs), while their semantic reasoning may exhibit vulnerabilities to perturbations in node text and graph structure under adversarial attack evaluation. Existing attacks typically construct textual and structural perturbations in separate stages guided by distinct realization intents. However, this separation results in attack-intent fragmentation, causing semantic incoherence between the two perturbation types, thereby undermining attack stealth and making them easier for GraphLLMs to identify and disregard, ultimately weakening attack effectiveness. To avoid attack-intent fragmentation, we propose the Graph Attack Primitive (GAP), an atomic semantic representation that unifies textual and structural perturbations under a coherent attack intent. By carefully calibrating the semantic composition of a GAP, we can achieve strong attack effectiveness while maintaining sufficient attack stealth. Furthermore, we introduce S-GAP (Stealthy GAP), a multi-agent framework that iteratively refines the semantic content of the GAP. The framework unifies GAP planning and realization and continuously refines this process through surrogate evaluation and textual feedback. Extensive experiments across four datasets, three GraphLLMs, and two attack settings show that S-GAP improves ASR over the strongest baselines by 7.02% on average while producing more relationally plausible perturbations, exposing critical vulnerabilities in current GraphLLMs and motivating more robust defenses.

摘要：

##### **HydraLLM: Adaptive Width Slicing for Elastic Text and Vision-Language Models**
2610.23455v1 by Oscar Kemme, Janek Haberer, Ali Hojjat, Olaf Landsiedel

LLMs and LVLMs are increasingly deployed across heterogeneous hardware, ranging from data-center GPUs to resource-constrained edge devices. Their fixed-size architectures limit the ability to reuse a single model across deployment settings with different compute, latency, and model-size targets. We introduce HydraLLM, an elastic MQA-based architecture. Its number of active attention heads determines the effective widths of the embeddings, attention projections, feed-forward layers, and the output head, forming a nested family of subnetworks within a single parameter set. We introduce a static-to-elastic conversion pipeline that, starting from a pre-trained model checkpoint, includes importance-driven weight reordering and stochastic subnetwork training with knowledge distillation. Every baseline we evaluate performs poorly (at most 15%), including frontier models given summarized or retrieval-augmented memory. A controlled study explains why: with oracle evidence delivered inside the trigger window GPT-5.3-Chat reaches 96%; letting it choose the timing drops it to 38%, and replacing oracle evidence with retrieval drops it to 15%. Content and timing are separately necessary, and missing either is catastrophic. Guided by this, we build ProPerAgent (Proactive Personalized Agent) with a plug-and-play harness that surfaces memory from a graph-organized store and gates it down to what is worth interrupting for, plus a lightweight RL stage that curbs early and repeated firing. 2610.21808v1 by Johannes Knittel, Alec Helbling, Hanspeter Pfister

2610.21807v1 by Hisatoshi Naganawa

Community question-answering platforms are important ecosystems for knowledge sharing, yet research on what makes an answer best or a question engaging has often focused on either individual lexical features or broad topics. However, striking a balance between plasticity and stability remains a significant challenge for intelligent systems. The human brain achieves exceptional balance, owing to various memory units that collaboratively encode and store information. 2610.21571v1 by Uiji Hwang, Richeng Xuan, Taeuk Kim

However, existing floorplan generation methods typically assume empty building boundaries as input, whereas renovation requires reasoning over existing floorplans. Furthermore, floorplan renovation and furniture layout are commonly addressed as separate tasks, overlooking their inherent spatial dependency. Consequently, practical residential design must preserve immutable structural components while transforming editable spatial elements. It must also optimize furniture placement under design objectives and physical constraints. To address these challenges, we formulate residential design as a design-point-guided conditional generation task and propose RenoLayout, a framework that translates a pre-design residential state together with a design-point set into an executable Geometry-as-Code program. To enable controllable supervision, we construct a Design Map comprising 123 fine-grained design points covering structural renovation and furniture layout. We further introduce Difference-Grounded Design-Point Annotation to derive design-point sets automatically from before--after design pairs with VLM-based consistency verification. RenoLayout is trained using a three-stage CPT--SFT--RL pipeline, progressively improving spatial reasoning, instruction following, and constraint compliance. We also introduce the RenoLayout Benchmark, a set of 200 real-world before--after renovation cases with complete furniture layouts. To the best of our knowledge, it is the first benchmark for unified residential design. These cases include design-critical structural and building-service constraints.

##### **Hierarchical Graph Convolutional Network Approach for Detecting Low-Quality Documents**
2610.21350v1 by Jaeyoung Lee, Joonwon Jang, Misuk Kim

Consistency within a document is a crucial feature indicative of its quality. Recently, within the vast amount of information produced across various media, there exists a significant number of low-quality documents that either lack internal consistency or contain content utterly unrelated to their headlines. Such low-quality documents induce fatigue in readers and undermine the credibility of the media source that provided them. Consequently, research to automatically detect these low-quality documents based on natural language processing is imperative. In this study, we introduce a hierarchical graph convolutional network (HGCN) that can detect internal inconsistencies within a document and incongruences between the title and body. Moreover, we constructed the Inconsistency Dataset, leveraging published news data and its meta-data, to train our model to detect document inconsistencies. Experimental results demonstrated that the HGCN achieved superior performance with an accuracy of 91.20% on our constructedInconsistency Dataset, outperforming other comparative models. Additionally, on the publicly available incongruent-related dataset, the proposed methodology demonstrated a performance of 92.00%, validating its general applicability. Finally, an ablation study further confirmed the significant impact of meta-data utilization on performance enhancement. We anticipate that our model can be universally applied to detect and filter low-quality documents in the real world.

##### **FedSkillGit: Federated Skill Evolution via Version Control**
2610.21348v1 by Boxun Lan, Jiamei Li, Weijian Li, Chengyu Yang, Zhuang Qi, Chengcheng Wan, Xiaohong Chen, Ming Hu

Large language model agents can improve their capabilities by evolving reusable natural-language skills from execution experience. In real-world deployments, this experience is distributed across data owners who cannot pool their raw data, which motivates federated skill evolution. The natural first design lets each client evolve the whole skill document and upload the result for server-side aggregation. That design is poorly matched to skill evolution for three reasons. An evolved document can still carry client-specific content distilled from private traces, repeated transmission of a nearly unchanged document spends communication on knowledge that the population already holds and the contribution of a single client is buried in a document that the server cannot meaningfully attribute. We propose FedSkillGit, a federated skill evolution framework that replaces full-document updates with commits. Each participating client submits a bounded set of patches over named rules together with its local experience, which summarizes what the round taught it, and the pair passes a two-stage privacy filter before it leaves the client. The server deduplicates the submitted patches and the local experience of all participants, updates a global experience document that only the optimizers read, and merges the surviving patches into a candidate skill. The candidate is distributed to the clients, which score it on their own validation sets and report the results, and it is published only when the reported scores improve on the current version. Experiments on three datasets and three language model families show that FedSkillGit outperforms state-of-the-art baselines by up to 22% and approaches centralized evolution without sharing raw client data.

##### **OpCredit: Operation-Aware Policy Learning for Agentic Knowledge Graph Question Answering**
2610.21345v1 by Yucheng Li, Mingyuan Li, Xiaxia Wang, Gong Cheng

Agentic knowledge graph question answering(KGQA) relies on sequences of executable operations, but trajectory-level rewards obscure their individual contributions. Successful trajectories may contain ineffective operations, while failed trajectories may contain useful ones. To assess individual operations, we propose OpCredit, an operationaware policy-learning framework that combines downstream outcome credit with immediate transition credit: Branch Outcome Credit (BOC) reuses ordinary rollouts to compare terminal task rewards observed after different operations under matched navigation contexts; Frontier Transition Credit (FTC) measures operation-induced changes in answer-conditioned frontier potential. The two complementary signals jointly supervise the corresponding decision spans during training, while inference uses only the learned policy and standard KG interface. Experiments on ComplexWebQuestions, WebQSP, and GrailQA across three backbones demonstrate consistent improvements in KGQA performance.

##### **Money-Mule Detection as a Relational Learning Problem: A Study on the RBIH NFPC Dataset**
2610.21314v1 by Owais Ahmad, Anirudh Deodhar

Money-mule networks move criminal funds through many small transfers, each of which looks ordinary on its own. What marks an account as a mule lies outside any single transfer: in the counterparties and addresses its network shares, and in the shape of its activity over time. Production systems remain largely rule-based and see neither, while most research classifies transactions rather than accounts, recovering account-level risk only by aggregating afterwards. We instead score the account directly, reading the bank's relational schema as a heterogeneous graph in which rows are nodes and foreign keys are edges. Each account is scored from a multi-hop neighbourhood using per-column encoders, a relative-time encoding and HeteroGraphSAGE message passing, with that neighbourhood restricted to rows predating the account's own pre-flag cutoff so that no model sees evidence recorded only after the account was flagged. On a five-year Reserve Bank Innovation Hub (RBIH) retail-banking dataset of 400 million transactions and 94{,}063 labelled accounts, 2.6\% of them confirmed mules, we compare against RelGNN, XGBoost, LightGBM and CatBoost trained on identical labels, splits and cutoffs. Our detector recovers nine in ten held-out mules at 83\% precision and a 0.5\% false-alarm rate, finding 115 more mules than the strongest tree ensemble given the same evidence. At the tuned thresholds the gap is mostly in recall: XGBoost and LightGBM lead across the first hundred alerts, but only the graph models hold their precision deep into the queue. Since the graph is the schema itself and every score is dated per account, the approach deploys on a bank's existing database without a custom feature graph build.

##### **Bias Control and Injection for Multi-Agent Aggregation**
2610.21290v1 by Sungjae Lee, Soheil Abbasloo, Jabin Koo, Jungseul Ok

Multi-agent reasoning with large language models (LLMs) raises a central question for collective decision-making: when agents disagree, whose decision should be trusted? Resolving such disagreements is difficult because agents' decisions are susceptible to non-semantic biases such as majority pressure and response-style preferences, which can obscure the quality of the underlying reasoning. Our analysis shows how bias can be both mitigated and exploited: bias control improves initial answer selection, while bias injection elicits decision instability that serves as a reliability signal. Building on these findings, we introduce a test-time aggregation framework that requires neither validation data nor prior knowledge of relative model strength. The framework implements bias control by presenting one style-normalized reasoning trace per distinct answer, and bias injection by placing each aggregating agent's initially selected answer in the minority. Overall, our framework achieves higher average accuracy and better token efficiency than competing non-oracle aggregation methods across three mathematical reasoning benchmarks and four heterogeneous LLM combinations. It also improves final-answer accuracy when applied to multi-agent debate and Mixture-of-Agents, without modifying their interaction or synthesis processes.

2610.21244v1 by Tianyu Chen, Xinyang Liu, Ruyi An, Zhendong Wang, Mingyuan Zhou

Large language models (LLMs) often make discrete decisions, such as flagging phishing or escalating to a human. 2610.21167v1 by Gaocheng Yang, Ruijie Tao, Kai Ye, Senhua Zhang, Zhengqin Zhu, Ziyang Li

Deep research agents are increasingly deployed to retrieve precise information from large corpora, yet multi-hop, long-horizon tasks remain challenging. To address this problem, we introduce GRADS, a graph-guided agentic data synthesis framework with explicit difficulty control. GRADS runs a closed loop of seed selection, evidence expansion, question--answer construction, and verification while maintaining a graph of the evidence and inference dependencies required to derive each answer. ##### **DST-Net: Decomposed Spatio-Temporal Network for Time Series Forecasting**
2610.21123v1 by Yaohua Zha, Hai Wu, Shu-Tao Xia

Series decomposition has emerged as a widely acknowledged paradigm in deep time series forecasting models. However, existing approaches predominantly employ a homogeneous modeling strategy for all subsequences, thereby overlooking the distinct inductive biases inherent in different components. We identify that the trend component exhibits dominant spatial coupling across channels but carries significant temporal redundancy, whereas the seasonal component is characterized by local temporal evolution but remains susceptible to attention distraction. To address this, we propose a novel disentangled architecture that tailors specific modeling strategies to align with the intrinsic properties of each component. For the trend branch, we design a Latent Graph Block, employing latent graph modeling to capture global topological dependencies and system-wide co-movements. Conversely, for the seasonal branch, we introduce a Gaussian Multi-scale Attention mechanism to mitigate attention distraction. By injecting learnable Gaussian priors, we impose structural constraints that compel the model to focus on multi-granular local contexts, effectively filtering out global noise and accurately capturing local evolutionary phases. Extensive experiments on multiple real-world benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of our proposed model.

##### **Planning Commitment of LLM under Partial Observability**
2610.21108v1 by Cassie Huang, Zhanwei Cao, Yeawon Lee, Nathalia Gomez, Zhuoyi Yang, Ian G. Harris, Tiffany Do, Jiayuan Mao, Li Zhang

LLM-based planning agents commit to plans of different lengths before interacting with a partially observable environment. Some aggressively commit to a long plan based on assumptions about unobserved states, while others conservatively plan only over what they have observed. Both behaviors emerge implicitly in pre-trained models, whether the LLM outputs a plan directly or generates a PDDL formulation for a classical solver. Aggressive commitment risks costly failures, while conservative commitment risks excessive LLM calls. This choice pits safety against efficiency, a trade-off that has been neither characterized systematically nor made controllable. We investigate aggressive and conservative LLM planners and formalizers on two partially observable domains, where priors are provided as either explicit statistics or common-sense knowledge. Across three LLMs, both strategies reach similar accuracy. Conservative methods achieve fewer failed attempts (safety) at the cost of more API calls (efficiency). Aggressive methods fail more often but replan efficiently only when priors are informative. Interestingly, analysis shows that LLMs treat the provided priors nearly as facts, which risks failure when priors are unreliable. We also explore hybrid strategies with tunable hyperparameters that adjust the balance between safety and efficiency.

Existing tabular distribution-shift benchmarks primarily construct out-of-distribution (OOD) settings by partitioning observations across naturally occurring domains. While such partitions may induce changes in P(Y | X), the resulting shifts are neither explicitly controlled nor isolated, and the evaluation distributions themselves are often not representative of the broader target population. We introduce INCONVENIENT, a benchmark designed to study robustness to convenience-sample bias. Rather than relying on naturally occurring domain partitions, INCONVENIENT directly perturbs feature--target relationships in the training distribution while preserving the original population distribution as the deployment environment. This setting more closely reflects real-world scenarios in which models are developed from unrepresentative samples and subsequently deployed on a broader population. The distinction is particularly relevant for large language models (LLMs), whose pretraining may encode population-level knowledge that extends beyond the relationships observed in a biased training dataset. Unlike existing distribution-shift benchmarks, INCONVENIENT provides explicit control over both the direction and severity of relationship shifts, enabling a systematic assessment of robustness to convenience sampling. ##### **SkillNEO: Non-Elitist Evolutionary Optimization of Agent Skills**

Recent multimodal generative systems have demonstrated similar capabilities: they can integrate multimodal context and synthesize visual outputs that depict changes in the world. To characterize this emerging trajectory, we organize model capabilities into a four-level hierarchy and discover that higher-level tasks remain relatively underexplored despite model progress. To bridge this gap, we explore model performance on multi-step world state prediction, a high-level task that requires models to forecast successive future world states from multimodal observations and diverse references. Such a task requires models to possess entangled understanding and generation capabilities, as well as extensive world knowledge to translate human action sequences, physical transformations, and abstract visual reasoning into coherent visual outputs. We introduce WorldCast, a benchmark comprising 320 manually designed cases across eight categories, and evaluate 12 representative models. The conducted experiments yield three key insights: (a) advances in frontier models are rapidly pushing the boundaries of intelligent world modeling, with the GPT-Image-2.5 System scoring 72.8 and demonstrating early-stage visual state prediction capabilities on real-world tasks; (b) WorldCast reveals substantial gaps between models: even Nano Banana Pro reaches only 42.8 and the best open-source model scores 20.1, with deficits in handling complex context, entangled references, and reasoning-heavy tasks; (c) strong generative models effectively draw on the capabilities of their underlying language models. These findings highlight the need for multimodal generative models that are capable of dynamic planning and reasoning while also being deeply unified to faithfully simulate real-world states.
